{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -33.21 M 0 -33.21 L 0 -22.14 L 11.07 -22.14 L 11.07 -33.21 Z M 11.07 -22.14}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\oplus\hbox to17pt{\vbox to25pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-16.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 11.07 L 11.07 11.07 L 11.07 0 Z M 11.07 11.07}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.07 0 M 11.07 0 L 11.07 11.07 L 22.14 11.07 L 22.14 0 Z M 22.14 11.07}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -11.07 M 0 -11.07 L 0 0 L 11.07 0 L 11.07 -11.07 Z M 11.07 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -22.14 M 0 -22.14 L 0 -11.07 L 11.07 -11.07 L 11.07 -22.14 Z M 11.07 -11.07}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\subset\mathcal{I}_{\ell}\,,

(3.1.31)

so that they are removed once
\mathcal{I}_{\ell}
is modded out
from the universal enveloping algebra of
\mathfrak{so}(2,d)
. This is,
in fact, a general pattern: Young diagrams with more than
two rows appearing in the decomposition of

\mathcal{U}\big(\mathfrak{so}(2,d)\big)
all belong to the ideal

\mathcal{I}_{\ell}
, and more specifically, to the ideal generated
by
V_{\mathsf{ABCD}}
. As a consequence, the higher spin algebra

\mathfrak{hs}_{\ell}
contains only Young diagrams with one or two rows.

(ii)

Second, the diagram
is obtained
as the product of the quadratic Casimir operator
\mathcal{C}_{2}

with the Lie algebra generators
M_{\mathsf{AB}}
.
Since, after modding out the ideal
\mathcal{I}_{\ell}
,
the value of
\mathcal{C}_{2}
is fixed, the adjoint representation
only appears with multiplicity one in
\mathfrak{hs}_{\ell}
.

One can immediately extract from the previous item
the following lesson: in order
for the quotient algebra
\mathfrak{hs}_{\ell}
to admit
a
multiplicity-free
decomposition under
\mathfrak{so}(2,d)
,
i.e. that all irreducible representations (irreps)
appear only once in the decomposition of
\mathfrak{hs}_{\ell}

under the adjoint action of
\mathfrak{so}(2,d)
,
the center of the universal enveloping algebra
has to be fixed. In other words, modding out the ideal

\mathcal{I}_{\ell}
should fix the values of all Casimir operators
(quadratic and higher), as the latter form a basis
of the center of
\mathcal{U}\big(\mathfrak{so}(2,d)\big)
.

Finally, since
\mathfrak{so}(2,d)
-module appearing
in the decomposition of the universal enveloping algebra

\mathcal{U}\big(\mathfrak{so}(2,d)\big)
are contained,
by definition, in tensor product of its adjoint
representation, all these irreps are characterised by
Young diagrams with an
even
number of boxes.
In view of the previous discussion, this means that
they are necessarily of the form
,
where difference between the number of boxes
in the first and in the second row is
even
.
This difference equals
t-1
where, as before,

t
is the depth of the partially massless field.
Modding out by the symmetric diagram

\vphantom{1}\smash{\scriptstyle 2\ell}

effectively removes all diagrams with
t>2\ell-1
,
as they would belong to the product of the former
with another diagram in the spectrum.

Howe duality in the Weyl algebra.

Now consider the Weyl algebra
\mathcal{A}_{2(d+2)}
generated by

\{Y^{\mathsf{A}}_{i}\}
where
i=\pm
, and with the Moyal–Weyl
star-product

f\star g=f\,\exp\big(\tfrac{\overleftarrow{\partial}}{\partial Y^\AlgInd{A}_i}\,
\eta^\AlgInd{AB} \epsilon_{ij}\,
\tfrac{\overrightarrow{\partial}}{\partial Y^\AlgInd{B}_j}\big)\,g\,,

(3.1.32)

where
\epsilon_{ij}
are the components of the canonical

2\times 2
symplectic matrix, as associative product.
Quadratic monomials in
Y^{\mathsf{A}}_{i}
, i.e. linear
combinations of the generators

K^{\mathsf{AB}}_{ij}:=\tfrac{1}{2}\,Y^{\mathsf{A}}_{i}Y^{\mathsf{B}}_{j}\,,

(3.1.33)

span a Lie subalgebra isomorphic to
sp\big(2(d+2),\mathbb{R}\big)
,

[K^{\mathsf{AB}}_{ij},K^{\mathsf{CD}}_{kl}]_{\star}=\eta^{\mathsf{BC}}\epsilon_{jk}\,K^{\mathsf{AD}}_{il}+\eta^{\mathsf{AC}}\epsilon_{ik}\,K^{\mathsf{BD}}_{jl}+\eta^{\mathsf{BD}}\epsilon_{jl}\,K^{\mathsf{AC}}_{ik}+\eta^{\mathsf{AD}}\epsilon_{il}\,K^{\mathsf{BC}}_{jk}\,,

(3.1.34)

where
[-,-]_{\star}
denotes the commutator with respect to
the star-product. The index structure on display here allows
one to easily identify two mutually commuting Lie subalgebras,

\mathfrak{so}(2,d)\oplus\mathfrak{sp}(2,\mathbb{R})\subset sp\big(2(d+2),\mathbb{R}\big)\,,

(3.1.35)

respectively generated by

M_{\mathsf{AB}}:=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\epsilon^{ij}\,Y^{\mathsf{A}}_{i}Y^{\mathsf{B}}_{j}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\tau_{ij}:=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\eta_{\mathsf{AB}}\,Y^{\mathsf{A}}_{i}Y^{\mathsf{B}}_{j}\,.

(3.1.36)

Such pairs of algebras are usually called reductive dual pairs,
or Howe dual pairs
[
187
,
188
,
189
]
(this will be discussed in more generality in the next subsection),
and can be used to construct a realisation of the type-A
ℓ
higher spin algebra in the Weyl algebra.

To construct such a realisation, we will first need to identify the centraliser

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{A}_{2(d+2)}}\big(\mathfrak{sp}(2,\mathbb{R})\big)

of
\mathfrak{sp}(2,\mathbb{R})
in the Weyl algebra
\mathcal{A}_{2(d+2)}
,
which is the space of elements annihilated by

[\tau_{ij},-]_{\star}=Y^{\mathsf{A}}_{(i}\,\epsilon_{j)k}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial Y^{\mathsf{A}}_{k}}\,,

(3.1.37)

or equivalently by the three operators

Y^{\mathsf{A}}_{+}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial Y^{\mathsf{A}}_{+}}-Y^{\mathsf{A}}_{-}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial Y^{\mathsf{A}}_{-}}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003ptY^{\mathsf{A}}_{+}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial Y^{\mathsf{A}}_{-}}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003ptY^{\mathsf{A}}_{-}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial Y^{\mathsf{A}}_{+}}\,.

(3.1.38)

The first operator imposes that elements in the centraliser
of
\mathfrak{sp}(2,\mathbb{R})
be of the same degree in
Y^{\mathsf{A}}_{+}

and
Y^{\mathsf{A}}_{-}
, while the other two operators
both impose that the coefficients of monomials
in
Y_{\pm}^{\mathsf{A}}
have the symmetry of a rectangular
Young diagram in the
\mathfrak{so}(2,d)
indices. In other words,

f(Y)\in\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{A}_{2(d+2)}}\big(\mathfrak{sp}(2,\mathbb{R})\big)\hskip 20.00003pt\Longleftrightarrow\hskip 20.00003ptf(Y)=\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}f_{\mathsf{A}(s-1),\mathsf{B}(s-1)}\,Y^{\mathsf{A}(s-1)}_{+}\,Y^{\mathsf{B}(s-1)}_{-}\,,

(3.1.39)

with
f_{\mathsf{A}(s-1),\mathsf{AB}(s-2)}=0
.
Note, however, that these tensors are still traceful,
and hence are
reducible
representations of
\mathfrak{so}(2,d)
.
Decomposing them into irreducible representations,
one would find all possible finite-dimensional irreps
of
\mathfrak{so}(2,d)
labelled by Young diagrams of the form

\hbox to78pt{\vbox to23.96pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-11.9084pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 15.22 L 106.54 15.22 L 106.54 0 Z M 106.54 15.22}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{27.26741pt}{2.27779pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -15.22 M 0 -15.22 L 0 0 L 60.88 0 L 60.88 -15.22 Z M 60.88 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{11.46185pt}{-8.5754pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{with}\hskip 20.00003pts\geq 1\,,\hskip 10.00002ptt\in 2\,\mathbb{N}+1\,,

(3.1.40)

i.e., all Young diagrams with two rows whose lengths differ
by an even number of boxes. Compared to the universal
enveloping algebra construction reviewed previously, we found ourselves with the same content as we do
after modding out
\mathcal{U}\big(\mathfrak{so}(2,d)\big)
by
the ideal generated by
V_{\mathsf{ABCD}}
. We also
face the same multiplicity problem: recall that we need
to fix the center of the universal enveloping algebra
in order to obtain a multiplicity-free spectrum. Here, the source of multiplicities is not only the center
of the universal enveloping algebra of
\mathfrak{so}(2,d)
,
but also that of
\mathfrak{sp}(2,\mathbb{R})
which is, by definition,
also contained in the centraliser of
\mathfrak{sp}(2,\mathbb{R})

in
\mathcal{A}_{2(d+2)}
. Fortunately, both problems
can be solved at once, thanks to the fact that the quadratic
Casimir operators of
\mathfrak{so}(2,d)
and
\mathfrak{sp}(2,\mathbb{R})
are related
via

\mathcal{C}_{2}[\mathfrak{so}(2,d)]+\mathcal{C}_{2}[\mathfrak{sp}(2,\mathbb{R})]=-\tfrac{1}{4}\,(d-2)(d+2)\,,

(3.1.41)

and similarly for higher order Casimir operators
(see e.g.
[
190
,
191
,
192
,
193
]
and
[
194
, Sec. 9]
for more details).
Since
\mathfrak{sp}(2,\mathbb{R})
only has one independent Casimir operator, it is sufficient to fix its value to also fix the values
of all Casimir operators of
\mathfrak{so}(2,d)
. In particular,
imposing

\mathcal{C}_{2}[\mathfrak{sp}(2,\mathbb{R})]=-(\ell-1)(\ell+1)\,,

(3.1.42)

sets the quadratic Casimir operator of
\mathfrak{so}(2,d)
to

\mathcal{C}_{2}[\mathfrak{so}(2,d)]=-\tfrac{1}{4}\,(d-2\ell)(d+2\ell)\,,

(3.1.43)

as it should in the type-A
ℓ
algebra
\mathfrak{hs}_{\ell}
.
Finally, notice that the diagrams of shape
(s-1,s-t)

with
t=2k+1
and
k\geq 0
appear as the
k
th trace
of rectangular diagrams, and that these traces are proportional
to
k
times the
\mathfrak{sp}(2,\mathbb{R})
generators. As a consequence, one can recover the partially massless higher spin algebra
as the quotient
6
6

6

Note that

\tau_{(i_{1}j_{1}}\dots\tau_{i_{\ell}j_{\ell})}
generate
the annihilator of the finite-dimensional

\mathfrak{sp}(2,\mathbb{R})
-irrep of highest weight
\ell-1
,
which is a reflection of the fact that the order-
\ell

singleton is Howe dual to this
\ell
-dimensional
irrep of
\mathfrak{sp}(2,\mathbb{R})

[
131
,
94
]
.

\mathfrak{hs}_{\ell}\cong\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{A}_{2(d+2)}}\big(\mathfrak{sp}(2,\mathbb{R})\big)\Big/\big\langle\tau_{(i_{1}j_{1}}\dots\tau_{i_{\ell}j_{\ell})}\oplus\mathcal{C}_{2}[\mathfrak{sp}(2,\mathbb{R})]-\tfrac{1}{2}\,(\ell-1)(\ell+1)\mathbf{1}\big\rangle\,,

(3.1.44)

as modding out the ideal generated by the elements

\tau_{(i_{1}j_{1}}\dots\tau_{i_{\ell}j_{\ell})}
guarantees
that only
\mathfrak{so}(2,d)
Young diagrams corresponding to
partially massless fields of depth
t=1,3,\dots,2\ell-1

remain. See e.g.
[
184
,
195
,
196
,
101
,
94
,
197
]

for more details on the construction of higher spin algebras
from the perspective of Howe duality.

3.1.3
Four-dimensional Specificities

Dual pairs and the Weyl–Clifford algebra.

Consider a set of bosonic
(Y^{A})
and fermionic
(\phi^{A}_{i})

oscillators, where the capital indices
A,B,\dots
take
2n
values
and the lower case indices
i,j,\dots
take
2p
values.
These oscillators are subject to the commutation
and anticommutation relations

\displaystyle\big[\hat{Y}^{A},\hat{Y}^{B}\big]

\displaystyle=2\,C^{AB}\,\mathbf{1}\,,

(3.1.45a)

\displaystyle\big\{\hat{\phi}^{A}_{i},\hat{\phi}^{B}_{j}\big\}

\displaystyle=2\,C^{AB}\epsilon_{ij}\mathbf{1}\,,

(3.1.45b)

where
C^{AB}=-C^{BA}
and
\epsilon_{ij}=-\epsilon_{ji}
are
two antisymmetric, non-degenerate matrices, with inverses given by

C^{AC}\,C_{BC}=\delta^{A}_{B}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\epsilon^{ik}\,\epsilon_{jk}=\delta^{i}_{j}\,.

(3.1.46)

We can therefore use these matrices to raise and lower indices,
which we will do using the convention

C^{AB}\,X_{B}=X^{A}\,,\hskip 20.00003ptX^{A}\,C_{AB}=X_{B}\,,

(3.1.47)

and similar convention for
\epsilon_{ij}
. The associative
algebra generated by these oscillators modulo the above
anti/commutation relations forms the Weyl–Clifford algebra

\mathcal{A}_{2n|4np}
, which is simply the tensor product
of the Weyl algebra generated by the bosonic oscillators,
and the Clifford algebra generated by the fermionic ones.

The elements quadratic in these oscillators
(modulo the previous anti/commutation relations),

K^{AB}:=\tfrac{1}{4}\big\{\hat{Y}^{A},\hat{Y}^{B}\big\}\,,\hskip 20.00003ptM^{AB}_{ij}:=\tfrac{1}{4}\,\big[\hat{\phi}^{A}_{i},\hat{\phi}^{B}_{j}\big]\,,\hskip 20.00003ptQ^{A|B}_{i}:=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\hat{Y}^{A}\,\hat{\phi}^{B}_{i}\,,

(3.1.48)

form a subalgebra isomorphic to
\mathfrak{osp}(4np|2n,\mathbb{R})
,
whose bosonic subalgebra
\mathfrak{o}(4np)\oplus\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})

is generated by
M^{AB}_{ij}
and
K^{AB}
,
and the odd/fermionic generators — the supercharges
— correspond to
Q^{A|B}_{i}
. Their anti/commutation
relations read

\displaystyle\big[K^{AB},K^{CD}\big]

\displaystyle=C^{BC}\,K^{AD}+C^{AC}\,K^{BD}+C^{BD}\,K^{AC}+C^{AD}\,K^{BC}\,,

(3.1.49a)

\displaystyle\big[M^{AB}_{ij},M^{CD}_{kl}\big]

\displaystyle=C^{BC}\epsilon_{jk}\,M^{AD}_{il}-C^{AC}\epsilon_{ik}\,M^{BD}_{jl}-C^{BD}\epsilon_{jl}\,M^{AC}_{ik}+C^{AD}\epsilon_{il}\,M^{BC}_{jk}\,,

(3.1.49b)

\displaystyle\big[K^{AB},Q^{C|D}_{i}\big]

\displaystyle=C^{BC}\,Q^{A|D}_{i}+C^{AC}\,Q^{B|D}_{i}\,,

(3.1.49c)

\displaystyle\big[M^{AB}_{ij},Q^{C|D}_{k}\big]

\displaystyle=C^{BD}\epsilon_{jk}\,Q^{C|A}_{i}-C^{AD}\epsilon_{ik}\,Q^{C|B}_{j}\,,

(3.1.49d)

\displaystyle\big\{Q^{A|C}_{i},Q^{B|D_{j}}\big\}

\displaystyle=C^{AB}\,M^{CD}_{ij}+C^{CD}\epsilon_{ij}\,K^{AB}\,.

(3.1.49e)

Note that the orthogonal algebra is presented
in a slightly unconventional basis here: one should
think of the pair of indices
(A,i)
on the generators

M^{AB}_{ij}
and
Q^{B|A}_{i}
as a single index
for the fundamental representation of
\mathfrak{o}(4np)
.
This is in accordance with the fact that only the first
capital index of the fermionic generators
Q^{A|B}_{i}

(the index ‘
A
’ here) is rotated by the
\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})

generators
K^{AB}
, whereas the second capital index
is rotated, along with the lower case index (the indices
‘
B
’ and ‘
i
’ here) are rotated together by the
\mathfrak{o}(4np)

generators
M^{AB}_{ij}
.

This unusual structure of indices for the
\mathfrak{o}(4np)

generators, which stems from the choice of indices
carried out by the fermionic oscillators
\phi^{A}_{i}
,
is motivated by the fact that we are interested in
singling out the pair of subalgebras

\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})\oplus\mathfrak{sp}(2p,\mathbb{R})\subset\mathfrak{o}(4np)\,,

(3.1.50)

generated by

J^{AB}:=\epsilon^{ij}\,M^{AB}_{ij}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\tau_{ij}:=C_{AB}\,M^{AB}_{ij}\,,

(3.1.51)

i.e. the generators obtained by contracting
those of
\mathfrak{o}(4np)
with the invariant tensors

\epsilon^{ij}
of
\mathfrak{sp}(2p,\mathbb{R})
, and

C_{AB}
of
\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
, respectively.
As is clear from the index structure of these generators,
these two subalgebras commute with one another,
i.e. they are contained in each other’s centraliser
in
\mathfrak{o}(4np)
, and in fact they are exactly
their respective centralisers.

An interlude on Howe duality.

Such a pair of subalgebras are usually called ‘dual pairs’
and have particularly interesting applications in representation
theory and physics. The most famous examples come from dual
pairs in a symplectic group
Sp(2N,\mathbb{R})
,
which are the central objects of study of Howe duality

[
187
,
188
]
. In this case, one can show
that the oscillator representation of
Sp(2N,\mathbb{R})
,
i.e. the Fock space generated by
N
pairs of bosonic
creation-annihilation operators admit a decomposition
into the direct sum of the tensor product of a representation
of each group of the dual pair.

Another variation on the same theme consists of
considering dual pairs in an
orthogonal
group,
say
O(2N)
.
This is precisely the case we are presented
with above, with the pair
\big(\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{R}),\mathfrak{sp}(2p,\mathbb{R})\big)\subset\mathfrak{o}(4np)
. For such dual pairs, the natural
representation of the orthogonal group is the Fock space
generated by
fermionic
pairs of creation-annihilation
operators. Indeed, bilinears in these operators define
a representation of the orthogonal group (or the double
cover thereof) on the fermionic Fock space, which can then
be decomposed into irreducible representations of
the dual pair of interest.
See e.g.
[
198
,
199
]
for more details
on this ‘skew-Howe’ duality.

Since we have both bosonic and fermionic oscillators
at hand, we can consider dual pairs in the orthosymplectic
group
[
200
,
201
,
202
]
. In our case,
the relevant pair is composed of
\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
, generated by

\displaystyle T^{AB}:=K^{AB}-\epsilon^{ij}\,M^{AB}_{ij}=\tfrac{1}{4}\,\big\{\hat{Y}^{A},\hat{Y}^{B}\big\}-\tfrac{1}{4}\,\big[\hat{\phi}^{A}_{i},\hat{\phi}^{Bi}\big]\,,

(3.1.52)

and satisfying the commutation relations

\displaystyle[T^{AB},\hat{Y}^{C}]

\displaystyle=C^{AC}\,\hat{Y}^{B}+C^{BC}\,\hat{Y}^{A}\,,

(3.1.53a)

\displaystyle[T^{AB},\hat{\phi}^{C}_{i}]

\displaystyle=C^{AC}\,\hat{\phi}^{B}_{i}+C^{BC}\,\hat{\phi}^{A}_{i}\,,

(3.1.53b)

\displaystyle[T^{AB},T^{CD}]

\displaystyle=C^{AC}\,T^{BD}+C^{AD}\,T^{BC}+C^{BD}\,T^{AC}+C^{BC}\,T^{AD}\,,

(3.1.53c)

and
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})
, generated by

Q_{i}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,C_{AB}\,\hat{Y}^{A}\,\hat{\phi}^{B}_{i}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\tau_{ij}\equiv\big\{Q_{i},Q_{j}\big\}=\tfrac{1}{4}\,C_{AB}\,[\hat{\phi}^{A}_{i},\hat{\phi}^{B}_{j}]\,,

(3.1.54)

obeying,

\displaystyle[\tau_{ij},Q_{k}]

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{kj}\,Q_{i}+\epsilon_{ki}\,Q_{j}\,,

(3.1.55a)

\displaystyle[\tau_{ij},\tau_{kl}]

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{ki}\tau_{jl}+\epsilon_{kj}\tau_{il}+\epsilon_{li}\tau_{jk}+\epsilon_{lj}\tau_{ik}\,.

(3.1.55b)

Casimir operators.

The quadratic Casimir operators for
\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})

and
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})
are respectively given by,

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{2}\big[\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})\big]=-\tfrac{1}{4}\,T_{AB}\,T^{AB}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{C}_{2}\big[\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})\big]=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,Q_{i}Q^{i}-\tfrac{1}{4}\,\tau_{ij}\tau^{ij}\,,

(3.1.56)

and a direct computation shows that, in the previously
described oscillator realisation, these Casimir operators
are related to one another via

\mathcal{C}_{2}\big[\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})\big]=\tfrac{n}{8}\,(2p-1)(2p+2n+1)-\mathcal{C}_{2}\big[\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})\big]\,.

(3.1.57)

In particular, for
n=2
and
p=\ell-1
,
one finds

\mathcal{C}_{2}\big[\mathfrak{sp}(4,\mathbb{R})\big]+\mathcal{C}_{2}\big[\mathfrak{osp}(1|2(\ell-1),\mathbb{R})\big]=-\tfrac{1}{4}\,(3-2\ell)(3+2\ell)\,,

(3.1.58)

this last number being the value of the quadratic
Casimir operator of
\mathfrak{so}(2,3)\cong\mathfrak{sp}(4,\mathbb{R})

on the module of the order-
\ell
scalar singleton.
This is a first hint that one may recover the type-A
ℓ

higher spin algebra as the centraliser of
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2(\ell-1),\mathbb{R})

in the Weyl–Clifford algebra, modulo
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2(\ell-1),\mathbb{R})

generators, as we shall prove in the next paragraphs.

Partially massless higher spin algebra.

In order to identify the type-A
ℓ
higher spin algebra,
let us first give an equivalent presentation of the Weyl–Clifford algebra in terms of symbols of the previous oscillators,
that we will denote by
Y^{A}
and
\phi^{A}_{i}
and which are
commuting and anticommuting, respectively. Their product
is the graded version of the previously discussed
Moyal--Weyl product
7
7

7

Note that, for a homogeneous
element
f\in\mathcal{A}_{2n|4np}
of degree
|f|
,
the left and right derivatives with respect to
Y^{A}

and
\phi^{A}_{i}
are related by

f\,\tfrac{\overleftarrow{\partial}}{\partial Y^{A}}=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial Y^{A}}f
and

f\,\tfrac{\overleftarrow{\partial}}{\partial\phi^{A}_{i}}=-(-1)^{|f|}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\phi^{A}_{i}}f
.

f\star g=f\,\exp\big(\tfrac{\overleftarrow{\partial}}{\partial Y^A}\,
C^{AB}\,\tfrac{\overrightarrow{\partial}}{\partial Y^B}
+\tfrac{\overleftarrow{\partial}}{\partial\phi^A_i}\,
C^{AB}\,\epsilon_{ij}\,
\tfrac{\overrightarrow{\partial}}{\partial\phi^B_j}\big)\,g\,,

(3.1.59)

where
f
and
g
are arbitrary polynomials in
Y^{A}
and
\phi^{A}_{i}
.
The symbols of the
\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
and
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})
generators
are simply

T^{AB}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,Y^{A}Y^{B}-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\epsilon^{ij}\,\phi^{A}_{i}\phi^{B}_{j}\,,\hskip 20.00003ptQ_{i}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,C_{AB}\,Y^{A}\phi^{B}_{i}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\tau_{ij}=\frac{1}{2}\,C_{AB}\,\phi^{A}_{i}\phi^{B}_{j}\,,

(3.1.60)

respectively.

Now let us characterise the centraliser of
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})

in
\mathcal{A}_{2n|4np}
, that is the space of elements
annihilated by

[Q_{i},-]_{\star}=\phi^{A}_{i}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial Y^{A}}-\epsilon_{ij}\,Y^{A}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\phi^{A}_{j}}\,,

(3.1.61)

where
[-,-]_{\star}
should be understood as the graded commutator
(i.e.
[f,g]_{\star}=f\star g-(-1)^{|f||g|}\,g\star f

for homogeneous elements of the Weyl–Clifford algebra
f
and
g
).
This condition is solved by considering any function
of the symbol of the
\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
generators
T^{AB}
,

f(Y^{A},\phi^{B}_{i})\in\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{A}_{2n|4np}}\big(\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})\big)\hskip 20.00003pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 20.00003ptf(Y^{A},\phi^{B}_{i})=f(T^{AB})\,,

(3.1.62)

since the symbols
T^{AB}
are characteristics of
the first order partial differential equations

[Q_{i},f]_{\star}=0
. Due to the fact that the
\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})

algebra contain a piece quadratic in the anticommuting variables

\phi^{A}_{i}
, the only possible diagram that can appear
when decomposing the centraliser of
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})

are those whose second row (and by extension, all rows
except the first one) are of length smaller than
2p
.
Indeed, upon splitting the
\mathfrak{sp}(2p,\mathbb{R})
indices as

i=(+\alpha,-\alpha)
with
\alpha=1,\dots,p
,
we have

\tfrac{1}{2}\,\epsilon^{ij}\,\phi^{A}_{i}\,\phi^{B}_{j}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{p}\varphi^{AB}_{\alpha}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 10.00002pt\varphi^{AB}_{\alpha}:=\phi^{(A}_{+\alpha}\,\phi^{B)}_{-\alpha}\,,

(3.1.63)

where

\varphi_{\alpha}^{(AB}\,\varphi^{CD)}_{\alpha}=0\,,

(3.1.64)

by virtue of the fact that
\phi^{A}_{i}
are anticommuting.
Note also that since the ‘building blocks’ of the centraliser
of
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})
are rank-
2
symmetric tensors of
\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
, all diagrams appearing will have an even number of boxes, and in particular, each row will be of even length. This means
that, for
n=2
, diagrams appearing in the centraliser
of
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})
will be of the form

\hbox to78pt{\vbox to24.11pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-12.05522pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

(3.1.65)

with
s\geq 1
and
t=1,3,\dots,2p+1
, so that upon setting

p=\ell-1
as before, we recover exactly the spectrum
of diagrams expected to appear in the higher spin algebra

\mathfrak{hs}_{\ell}
(in the
\mathfrak{sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
basis). However, these diagrams
are not traceless in
\mathfrak{sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
sense a priori: consider
for instance the product of two
\mathfrak{sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
generators,
which can be projected onto a totally symmetric part,

T^{ABCD}:=T^{(AB}\,T^{CD)}\hskip 20.00003pt\longleftrightarrow\hskip 20.00003pt\hbox to33pt{\vbox to9pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-0.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

(3.1.66)

and a piece with the symmetry of a ‘window-shaped’ diagram,

T^{AB,CD}:=T^{AB}\,T^{CD}-T^{A(C}\,T^{D)B}\hskip 20.00003pt\longleftrightarrow\hskip 20.00003pt\hbox to17pt{\vbox to17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-8.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.07 0 M 11.07 0 L 11.07 11.07 L 22.14 11.07 L 22.14 0 Z M 22.14 11.07}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -11.07 M 0 -11.07 L 0 0 L 11.07 0 L 11.07 -11.07 Z M 11.07 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.07 -11.07 M 11.07 -11.07 L 11.07 0 L 22.14 0 L 22.14 -11.07 Z M 22.14 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,.

(3.1.67)

While the first one, the totally symmetric part,
is trivially traceless, the second one is not since

C_{BC}\,T^{AB,CD}\ \propto\ 2\,\epsilon^{ij}\,\phi^{[A}_{i}Y^{D]}\,Q_{j}-\epsilon^{ij}\epsilon^{kl}\,\phi^{A}_{i}\phi^{D}_{k}\,\tau_{jl}\,,

(3.1.68)

does not vanish identically, but is proportional to
the
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2(\ell-1),\mathbb{R})
generators. This is in fact a general
feature, namely all traces are proportional to these generators.
Indeed, taking a trace in the
\mathfrak{sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
sense
means contracting the capital latin indices
A,B,\dots

with the invariant tensor
C_{AB}
, which thereby produces
the generators
Q_{i}
and
\tau_{ij}
. Consequently, we can remove
traces by modding out the centraliser of
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2(\ell-1),\mathbb{R})

by the ideal generated by
Q_{i}
and
\tau_{ij}
, and thereby
obtain the type-A
ℓ
higher spin algebra in four dimensions
as the quotient
8
8

8

Note that this definition
also works for
\ell=1
, even though this case may seem degenerate
at first glance. Indeed, in this case the Howe dual algebra
becomes trivial, which is simply a consequence of the fact
the relevant Howe dual
group
is the finite group

\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. This group acts on the Weyl algebra
by reflections
Y^{A}\to-Y^{A}
, so that its centraliser
is nothing but the
even
subalgebra, the subalgebra
of polynomials in an even number of
Y^{A}
’s.

\mathfrak{hs}_{\ell}\cong\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{A}_{4|8(\ell-1)}}\big(\mathfrak{osp}(1|2(\ell-1),\mathbb{R})\big)\Big/\big\langle Q_{i}\oplus\tau_{ij}\big\rangle\,.

(3.1.69)

The main difference compared to the realisation reviewed
in the previous section is that here, the ‘order’
\ell

of the theory is no longer controlled by choosing different
ideal to mod out from the centraliser of the Howe dual algebra,
but by the choice of the Howe dual algebra itself. This allows
us to slightly simplify the identification of the type-A
ℓ

higher spin algebra in four dimensions with respect to
the arbitrary dimension construction.

Given that
\mathfrak{hs}_{\ell}
is the symmetry algebra
of the order-
\ell
scalar singleton, and having found
a realisation of it within the Weyl–Clifford algebra,
it is natural to seek a realisation of the order-
\ell
singleton
in the Fock space generated by the bosonic and fermionic oscillators
used above, which we will do in the next section.

3.2
Higher order singleton module

The Weyl–Clifford algebra generated by the oscillators

\hat{Y}^{A}
and
\hat{\phi}^{A}_{i}
introduced
in Section
3
naturally acts on the Fock space
generated by
n
pairs of bosonic creation-annihilation
operators,

[\mathsf{a}_{a},\bar{\mathsf{a}}^{b}]=\delta^{b}_{a}\,\mathbf{1}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\bar{\mathsf{a}}^{a}:=(\mathsf{a}_{a})^{\dagger}\,,\hskip 20.00003pta,b,\dots=1,\dots,n\,,

(3.2.1)

and
2np
fermionic ones,

\{\mathsf{c}^{i}_{a},\bar{\mathsf{c}}_{j}^{b}\}=\delta^{i}_{j}\,\delta^{b}_{a}\,\mathbf{1}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\bar{\mathsf{c}}^{a}_{i}:=(\mathsf{c}_{a}^{i})^{\dagger}\,,\hskip 20.00003pti,j,\dots=1,\dots,2p\,.

(3.2.2)

In fact, the Weyl–Clifford algebra is the algebra
of endomorphisms of this Fock space.
Bilinears in these creation-annihilation operators
form a Lie subalgebra isomorphic to
\mathfrak{osp}(2n|4np,\mathbb{R})
,
which contain the dual pair
\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})\oplus\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})

discussed previously. Introducing the notation,

v\cdot w:=\epsilon^{ij}\,v_{i}\,w_{j}=\epsilon_{ij}\,v^{i}\,w^{j}\,,

(3.2.3)

for the contraction of the
\mathfrak{sp}(2p,\mathbb{R})
indices,
the generators of
\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
are given by

T^{ab}:=\bar{\mathsf{a}}^{a}\,\bar{\mathsf{a}}^{b}-\bar{\mathsf{c}}^{a}\cdot\bar{\mathsf{c}}^{b}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003ptT_{ab}:=\mathsf{a}_{a}\,\mathsf{a}_{b}-\mathsf{c}_{a}\cdot\mathsf{c}_{b}\,,

(3.2.4a)

T^{a}{}_{b}:=\bar{\mathsf{a}}^{a}\,\mathsf{a}_{b}+\bar{\mathsf{c}}^{a}\cdot\mathsf{c}_{b}+\tfrac{1-2p}{2}\,\delta^{a}_{b}\,\mathbf{1}\,,

(3.2.4b)

while the generators of
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})
read

Q_{i}:=\tfrac{1}{2}\,(\bar{\mathsf{c}}^{a}_{i}\,\mathsf{a}_{a}-\bar{\mathsf{a}}^{a}\,\epsilon_{ij}\,\mathsf{c}_{a}^{j})\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\tau_{ij}:=\epsilon_{k(i}\,\bar{\mathsf{c}}_{j)}^{a}\,\mathsf{c}^{k}_{a}\,.

(3.2.5)

Now let us isolate the
\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
representation
dual to the trivial irrep of
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})
. Doing so
amounts to finding states in the Fock space
which are annihilated by the action of the
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})

supercharges, i.e.

Q_{i}\,f(\bar{\mathsf{a}},\bar{\mathsf{c}})\lvert 0\rangle=0\,,

(3.2.6)

which is solved by

f(\bar{\mathsf{a}},\bar{\mathsf{c}})=f(T^{ab})\,,

(3.2.7)

that is, any function of the
\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
raising operators

T^{ab}
. Since the vacuum
\lvert 0\rangle
of the Fock space
is
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})
-invariant, it defines a lowest weight vector
for the dual
\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
-module, with weight

(\underbrace{\tfrac{1-2p}{2},\dots,\tfrac{1-2p}{2}}_{n\,\text{times}})\,,

(3.2.8)

with respect to the Cartan subalgebra spanned by
the generators
T^{a}{}_{a}
(no summation implied).
The subspace of homogeneous polynomials of degree
k

in
T^{ab}
is preserved by the action of the
\mathfrak{u}(n)
subalgebra
generated by
T^{a}{}_{b}
(since the latter preserve
the number of creation/annihilation operators). The decomposition
of these subspaces into irreducible representations of
\mathfrak{u}(n)

consists of all Young diagram with
2k
boxes, whose rows
are all of even length and such that the second row is of length
at most
2p
. In particular, for
n=2
, the lowest weight

\mathfrak{sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
-module dual to the trivial
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})
-representation
admits the decomposition

\mathcal{D}_{\mathfrak{sp}(4,\mathbb{R})}\big(\tfrac{1-2p}{2},\tfrac{1-2p}{2}\big)\cong\bigoplus_{s=0}^{\infty}\bigoplus_{k=0}^{p}\big[\tfrac{1-2p}{2}+2s+2k,\tfrac{1-2p}{2}+2k\big]_{\mathfrak{u}(2)}\,,

(3.2.9)

under the maximal compact subalgebra
\mathfrak{u}(2)\subset\mathfrak{sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
.
Taking into account the isomorphism

[\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}]_{\mathfrak{u}(2)}\cong\big[\tfrac{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}{2},\tfrac{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}{2}\big]_{\mathfrak{so}(2)\oplus\mathfrak{so}(3)}\,,

(3.2.10)

between finite-dimensional irreps of
\mathfrak{u}(2)

and
\mathfrak{so}(2)\oplus\mathfrak{so}(3)
and setting
p=\ell-1
,
this decomposition matches the one of the order-
\ell

singleton module

\mathcal{D}_{\mathfrak{so}(2,3)}\big(\tfrac{3-2\ell}{2},0\big)\cong\bigoplus_{s=0}^{\infty}\bigoplus_{t=1,3,\dots}^{2\ell-1}\big[\tfrac{3-2\ell}{2}+t-1+s,s\big]_{\mathfrak{so}(2)\oplus\mathfrak{so}(3)}

(3.2.11)

in three dimensions (see e.g.
[
203
, Sec. 3.4.2]
).

A word about non-unitarity.

Let us conclude this section by commenting
on the non-unitarity of these modules. Recall that
higher-order singletons are lowest weight irreps
of
\mathfrak{so}(2,d)
, which can therefore be described
by its lowest weight vector
\ket{\phi}
, obeying

(D-\Delta_{\phi})\ket{\phi}=0\,,\hskip 20.00003ptJ_{ab}\ket{\phi}=0\,,\hskip 20.00003ptK_{a}\ket{\phi}=0\,,

(3.2.12)

where
D
,
J_{ab}
and
K_{a}
are the dilation,
Lorentz, and special conformal transformation generators.
All states of the modules are obtained
by repeated application of the translation generators

P_{a}
on
\ket{\phi}
. Using the relations

[K_{a},P_{b}]=\eta_{ab}\,D-M_{ab}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt[D,P_{a}]=P_{a}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt[M_{ab},P_{c}]=2\,\eta_{c[b}\,P_{a]}\,,

(3.2.13)

one finds

K_{a}\,P^{2}\,\ket{\phi}=2\,\big(\Delta_{\phi}-\tfrac{d-2}{2}\big)\,P_{a}\ket{\phi}\,,

(3.2.14)

and with
P_{a}^{\dagger}=K_{a}
, this implies

\|P^{2}\,\ket{\phi}\|^{2}=2d\,\Delta_{\phi}\,\big(\Delta_{\phi}-\tfrac{d-2}{2}\big)\,\langle\phi\!\mid\!\phi\rangle\,.

(3.2.15)

The above identities tells us that
P^{2}\,\ket{\phi}

is singular and null for
\Delta_{\phi}=\tfrac{d-2}{2}
,
while for
\Delta_{\phi}<\tfrac{d-2}{2}
it is not singular
but acquires a negative norm.
For the order-
\ell
singleton,

\Delta_{\phi}=\tfrac{d-2\ell}{2}
and hence the presence
of
P^{2}\ket{\phi}
is one of the first indications
that the module is non-unitary.

Now coming back to our construction,
it may be surprising that such a non-unitary module
can be realised in a Fock space, which is usually
itself a unitary module (for the Heisenberg algebra,
or its supersymmetric version relevant here).
A first consistency check is that this negative
norm state
P^{2}\ket{\phi}
is indeed present,
since we recover the correct
\mathfrak{u}(2)
decomposition.
More importantly, the Hermitian conjugation

does not preserve
the
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})

generators in this realisation, which is why
we have a non-unitary module in a Fock space.

Higher order spinor singleton?

Note that one could look for other representation
of
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})
than the trivial one. For instance,
the ‘next-to-simplest’ representation is of dimension

2p+1\equiv 2\ell-1
and splits into a direct sum
of
\mathfrak{sp}(2p,\mathbb{R})
irreps, the trivial and the vector
(or fundamental) one. It can be realised
in the Fock space considered here as the subspace
with basis

\bar{\mathsf{a}}^{a}\ket{0}\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\bar{\mathsf{c}}^{a}_{i}\ket{0}\,,

(3.2.16)

which are indeed, for
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})
,
a scalar and a vector respectively.
This subspace is preserved by the action
of
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})
since

Q_{i}\,\bar{\mathsf{a}}^{a}\ket{0}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\bar{\mathsf{c}}^{a}_{i}\ket{0}\,,\hskip 20.00003ptQ_{i}\,\bar{\mathsf{c}}_{j}^{a}\ket{0}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\epsilon_{ij}\,\bar{\mathsf{a}}^{a}\ket{0}\,,

(3.2.17)

while the
\mathfrak{sp}(2p,\mathbb{R})
generators
\tau_{ij}

merely rotate this states, as expected.
As usual in the context of Howe duality,
this representation appears with a
multiplicity
,
as indicated by the fact that the above basis vectors
also carry an
\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
index. In fact,
as in the case of the trivial representation,
any state obtained from the above basis vectors
by the action of
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})
-invariant operators,
which are generated by the Howe dual algebra
\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
,
will not change the representation. In other words,
this finite-dimensional representation of
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})

appears with
infinite multiplicity
in the Fock space,
but this feature is merely the reflection of the fact
that it is Howe dual to a lowest weight module
of
\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
, which is infinite-dimensional.

The lowest weight
\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
-module in question
is induced by the lowest
u(n)
-irrep spanned by
the state
\bar{\mathsf{a}}^{a}\ket{0}
and
\bar{\mathsf{c}}^{a}_{i}\ket{0}
,
and generated by the action of the raising operators
T^{ab}
.
The lowest weight reads

\big(\tfrac{3-2p}{2},\tfrac{1-2p}{2},\dots,\tfrac{1-2p}{2}\big)\,,

(3.2.18)

which, in the case of
\mathfrak{sp}(4,\mathbb{R})\cong\mathfrak{so}(2,3)
,
corresponds to the lowest weight

\big[\tfrac{3-2p}{2},\tfrac{1-2p}{2}\big]_{\mathfrak{u}(2)}=\big[2-\ell,\tfrac{1}{2}\big]_{\mathfrak{so}(2)\oplus\mathfrak{so}(3)}\,,

(3.2.19)

whose components are respectively the conformal weight
and spin of the spinor singleton of order
\ell

(i.e. a free spinor
\psi
subject to the higher order
Dirac equation
{\partial}^{2\ell-1}\psi\approx 0

as recalled in the Introduction). In other words,
we find that the higher order spinor singleton

\mathcal{D}_{\mathfrak{sp}(4,\mathbb{R})}(\tfrac{3-2p}{2},\tfrac{1-2p}{2}\big)\cong\mathcal{D}_{\mathfrak{so}(2,3)}\big(2-\ell,\tfrac{1}{2}\big)\,,

(3.2.20)

is Howe dual to the finite-dimensional
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})

representation made out of the trivial and vector

\mathfrak{sp}(2p,\mathbb{R})
-irreps.

This therefore begs the question: can we find the type-B
ℓ

higher spin algebra in our construction? To do so,
one would need to quotient the centraliser of
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})

in the Weyl–Clifford algebra by a different ideal
than the one generated by
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})
. Indeed,
we saw previously that the scalar singleton is Howe dual
to the
trivial
representation of
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})
,
and hence the full algebra is the annihilator
of this representation. The quotient by
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})

should be understood as the quotient by the annihilator
of this trivial representation—as recalled above
when we discussed the definition of the type-A
ℓ

algebra in arbitrary dimensions. Having this framework
in mind, we should quotient the centraliser of
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})

by the annihilator of its
(2\ell-1)
-dimensional irrep
in order to obtain the type-B
ℓ
higher spin algebra.
Schematically, this means modding out by higher powers
of the
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})
generators, which in turn amounts
to keeping some of the traces in the diagrams (
3.1.65
),
as may be expected to reproduce the spectrum
of the type-B
ℓ
algebra. Such an analysis is however
beyond the scope of this chapter, and we leave for potential
future work.

3.3
Formal partially massless higher spin gravity

Having built an oscillator realisation of the higher spin
algebra
\mathfrak{hs}_{\ell}
in four dimensions, we will now use it
to try and construct an interacting theory
of partially massless higher spin fields.

The most common way of constructing formal higher spin gravities
is to consider a gauge connection
\omega
of the relevant
higher spin algebra
\mathfrak{hs}
, together with a zero-form
C

taking value in a module of this algebra
(see e.g.
[
19
,
204
,
184
,
195
,
171
,
172
,
131
,
101
,
205
,
206
]
).
This data is associated with the coordinates
on a
Q
-manifold, which we denote by the same symbols,
and whose (co)homological vector field
Q
encodes
the interactions. More precisely, one is then charged
with constructing equations of motion

\displaystyle d\omega

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}(\omega,\omega)+\mathcal{V}(\omega,\omega,C)+\dots\,,

(3.3.1)

\displaystyle dC

\displaystyle=\mathcal{U}(\omega,C)+\mathcal{U}(\omega,C,C)+\dots\,,

(3.3.2)

where
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{U}
are the component of
Q
,
and the initial data for the deformation problem reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(a,b)

\displaystyle=a\star b\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(a,u)

\displaystyle=a\star u-u\star\pi(a)\,,

(3.3.3)

where
\pi
is an anti-involution of the higher spin algebra.
At this point it is convenient to define

{}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\mathfrak{hs}=\mathfrak{hs}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}_{2}
,
where
\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\{1,\pi\}
. In practice,
one adds an element
k
such that
k^{2}=1

and
k\star a\star k=\pi(a)
.

Under some fairly general assumptions, one can show that
the problem of constructing the
A_{\infty}
-algebra
underlying the
Q
-manifold reduces to a much simpler problem
of deforming
{}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\mathfrak{hs}
as an associative algebra
[
207
,
208
,
197
,
206
]
.
Moreover, often times it is easy to see that

{}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\mathfrak{hs}
can be deformed and even construct
such a deformation, which we call
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{u}}
, explicitly.
Once
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{u}}
is available, there is an explicit
procedure to construct all vertices. For example,

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(a,b,u)

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}(a,b)\star\pi(u)\,,

(3.3.4)

where
\phi_{1}
is a (Hochschild)
2
-cocycle that determines
the first order deformation of
{}^{\mathbb{Z}_{2}}\mathfrak{hs}

to
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{u}}
:

\displaystyle a\circ b

\displaystyle=a\star b+u\,\phi_{1}(a,b)k+\mathcal{O}(u^{2})\,.

(3.3.5)

It has to be noted that the above form of the vertices
is non-minimal: the equations, in general,
‘mix’ different spins even at the free level.
One therefore needs to find a suitable field redefinition
to bring the vertex in its ‘minimal’ form
wherein such mixing are absent.

The deformed algebra
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{u}}
is defined from
\mathfrak{hs}
,
the latter being usually obtained via either one
of the following constructions:

(a)

A quotient of the universal enveloping algebra

\mathcal{U}\big(\mathfrak{so}(d,2)\big)
by a two-sided ideal
\mathcal{I}

(in most cases called the Joseph ideal), which corresponds
to the annihilator of a given irreducible
\mathfrak{so}(2,d)
-module,
e.g.
[
183
,
184
,
94
,
209
,
210
,
211
]
;

(b)

Using an oscillator realisation, wherein
one embeds
\mathfrak{so}(2,d)
and its enveloping algebra
in a Weyl(–Clifford) algebra and typically obtain
\mathfrak{hs}

as the quotient of the centraliser of a Howe dual
algebra, as discussed above for the type-A
ℓ
algebra,
as well as in
[
184
,
195
,
212
,
101
,
213
,
210
]

and references therein;

(c)

Via the
quasi-conformal
realisation,
which consists in explicitly solving the defining relations
of the (Joseph) ideal mentioned previously, see e.g.

[
214
,
215
,
210
,
216
]
.

The first order deformation defined by the
2
-cocycle

\phi_{1}
makes its presence felt already at the free level.
Indeed, linearizing the above equations around an (A)dS
d+1

background,

\omega_{0}=e^{a}\,P_{a}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\varpi^{a,b}\,L_{ab}\,,\hskip 20.00003ptC_{0}=0\,,\hskip 20.00003ptd\omega_{0}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,[\omega_{0},\omega_{0}]=0\,,

(3.3.6)

their first order in the field fluctuations
should reproduce the free field equations
for partially massless fields in the frame-like formalism

[
58
]
, whose schematic form reads

R[\omega_{1}]^{a(s-1),b(s-t)}=e_{c}\wedge e_{d}\,C_{1}^{a(s-1)c,b(s-t)d}\,,\hskip 20.00003ptR[\omega_{1}]^{a(s-1-m),b(s-t-n)}=0\,,

(3.3.7)

where
R[\omega_{1}]^{a(s-1-m),b(s-t-n)}=\nabla\omega_{1}^{a(s-1-m),b(s-t-n)}+\dots
,
with
\omega_{1}
the first order fluctuations
of a
1
-form valued in
\mathfrak{hs}_{\ell}
.
More specifically, the components of
1
-form

\omega_{1}
take values in the finite-dimensional
representations of
\mathfrak{so}(2,d)
labelled by the two-row
Young diagrams of the form

\hbox to67pt{\vbox to23.96pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-11.9084pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{11.46185pt}{-8.5754pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 20.00003ptt=1,3,\dots,2\ell-1\,,\hskip 10.00002pts=t,t+1,\dots,

(3.3.8)

which corresponds to generators of the form

M_{\mathsf{A}(s-1),\mathsf{B}(s-t)}=\underbrace{M_{\mathsf{AB}}\cdots M_{\mathsf{AB}}}_{s-t}\,\underbrace{M_{\mathsf{A}}{}^{\mathsf{C}}\,M_{\mathsf{AC}}\cdots M_{\mathsf{A}}{}^{\mathsf{C}}\,M_{\mathsf{AC}}}_{\frac{t-1}{2}}+\dots\,,

(3.3.9)

where the dots denote terms ensuring
that the right hand side has the symmetry
of the above Young diagram, and is traceless.
The first order fluctuation of the zero-form
takes values in a representation of
\mathfrak{hs}_{\ell}
,
usually called the ‘twisted-adjoint representation’.
9
9

9

Although it may be more relevant
to think of it as a coadjoint module

[
29
, App. B]
.

This module of the type-A
ℓ
algebra is defined
on the same vector space as
\mathfrak{hs}_{\ell}
,
but where the latter acts via a ‘twisted commutator’

\mathcal{U}(\omega_{0},C_{1})=\omega_{0}\star C_{1}-C_{1}\star\pi(\omega_{0})\,,

(3.3.10)

hence the name of this representation. The zero-forms
can therefore be expanded in a basis of generators
of the (partially massless) higher spin algebra

[
184
,
203
]
. Typically, the Weyl tensor

C^{a(s),b(s-t+1)}
, for the spin-
s

and depth-
t
partially massless field
is the component of
C_{1}
along the generator
of
\mathfrak{hs}_{\ell}
which schematically reads,

\underbrace{L_{ab}\dots L_{ab}}_{s-t+1}\underbrace{P_{a}\dots P_{a}}_{t-1}P^{2\ell-t-1}+\dots\,,

(3.3.11)

where we separated generators of
\mathfrak{so}(2,d)

into those of the Lorentz subalgebra
\mathfrak{so}(1,d)
,
denoted by
L_{ab}
, and the transvection
(or AdS-translation) generators denoted by
P_{a}
.

The low spin (
s=1
and
2
) components
of these fluctuations are given by

\omega_{1}=A\cdot\mathbf{1}+h^{a}\,P_{a}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\omega^{ab}\,L_{ab}+\dots\,,\hskip 20.00003ptC_{1}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,F^{a,b}\,\mathcal{M}_{a,b}+\dots\,,

(3.3.12)

where, to keep this discussion fairly general,
we denoted by
\mathcal{M}_{a,b}
the generator of
\mathfrak{hs}

along which one finds the Maxwell tensor,
independently of the higher spin algebra of interest.
In the type-A case, it would simply be
\mathcal{M}_{a,b}=L_{ab}
,
while in the type-A
ℓ
case, it would be of the form

L_{ab}P^{2(\ell-1)}+(\dots)
instead.
When comparing (
3.3.1
)
to the previous free equations of motion,
e.g. in the spin-
1
sector,

dA=e_{a}\wedge e_{b}\,F^{a,b}+\dots\,,

(3.3.13)

we can deduce that
\mathcal{V}
yields

\mathcal{V}(P_{a},P_{b};\mathcal{M}_{c,d})=2\,\eta_{a[c}\,\eta_{d]b}\,\mathbf{1}+\dots\,,

(3.3.14)

when evaluated on
P_{a}\otimes P_{b}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{c,d}
.
From (
3.3.4
), we know that the dots
in the previous equation originate from the expression

\phi_{1}(P_{a},P_{b})\star\mathcal{M}_{a,b}=2\,\eta_{a[c}\,\eta_{d]b}\,\mathbf{1}+\dots\,,

(3.3.15)

modulo the field-redefinition
bringing the vertex in its ‘minimal’ form.
In other words, the product of
\phi_{1}(P_{a},P_{b})

and the generator that corresponds to the Maxwell tensor
must contain the unit of
\mathfrak{hs}
. Let us note
that
\phi_{1}(P_{a},P_{b})
is also the simplest term
to probe the deformation since
\phi_{1}(\mathbf{1},\bullet)=0

and
\phi_{1}(L_{ab},\bullet)=0
. The first condition
means that the unit is not deformed and the second one
protects Lorentz symmetry.

Recalling that
\mathfrak{hs}
has an invariant trace
\tr

(defined as the projection onto the unit),
the above condition can also be rewritten as

\displaystyle\tr\big(\phi_1(P_a, P_b) \star\Maxwell_{a,b}\big)\neq 0\,.

(3.3.16)

Since the basis of any higher spin algebra
\mathfrak{hs}

can be decomposed into finite-dimensional

\mathfrak{so}(d,2)
-modules, and that the trace respects
\mathfrak{so}(d,2)
,
different generators are orthogonal to each other.
As a result, we have to have

\displaystyle\phi_{1}(P_{a},P_{b})\,

\displaystyle\propto\,\eta_{ab}+T_{ab}+\dots

\displaystyle\tr\big(T_{ab} \star\Maxwell_{a,b})\neq 0\,,

(3.3.17)

where
T_{ab}
is a generator of
{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{u}}

that
deforms
the commutator
[P_{a},P_{b}]
.
In other words, the generator
T_{ab}
of this deformation
is a multiple of the
dual
of the Maxwell tensor
generator
\mathcal{M}_{a,b}
. In order to define

{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{A}}_{u}}
, one needs to define

[P_{a},P_{b}]=L_{ab}+uk\,T_{ab}

and deform the Joseph ideal accordingly.

The Maxwell tensor (and the whole decomposition)
can be found by decomposing the twisted-adjoint action

\{P_{a},\bullet\}
of translations on
C
.
The adjoint of the Lorentz algebra
may appear with multiplicity greater than
1

(this happens for instance in the type-B or Type-A
ℓ
,

\ell>1
, cases). The Maxwell equations
should have the form

\displaystyle\nabla F^{a,b}

\displaystyle=h_{c}F^{ac,b}

(3.3.18)

\displaystyle\nabla F^{ab,c}

\displaystyle\propto h^{(a}F^{b),c}-\tfrac{1}{d}\,h_{\times}\,\big(\eta^{ab}F^{c,\times}-\eta^{c(a}F^{b),\times}\big)+\dots\,,

(3.3.19)

where the first line comes from the anticommutator

\{P_{m},\mathcal{M}_{ac,b}\}
, where
\mathcal{M}_{ab,c}

is a traceless and hook-symmetric generator of the form

\mathcal{M}_{a,b}P_{c}+(\dots)
, and in the second line
from
\{P_{m},\mathcal{M}_{a,b}\}
. Most importantly,
F^{a,b}

must not contribute anywhere else. The second equation
means that, at the algebra level, one finds

\displaystyle\{P_{(a},\mathcal{M}_{b),c}\}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}_{ab,c}\,.

(3.3.20)

which implies that
\{P^{a},\mathcal{M}_{a,b}\}=0
,
and hence this anticommutator must be a part
of the two-sided ideal defining
\mathfrak{hs}
.
This is indeed the case for the Type-A algebra,
whose Joseph ideal contains
\{P^{m},L_{mb}\}=0
.
For the type-A
ℓ
case,
\mathcal{M}_{a,b}
must be
in the adjoint representation that sits inside
the subspace of monomials of order
\ell-1

in the
\mathfrak{so}(2,d)
generator (i.e. one degree less
than the generator
\mathcal{J}_{\mathsf{A}(2\ell)}

of the Joseph ideal).

Probing deformation through cycles.

Cocycles are more complicated to derive than cycles
since cocycles are defined on the whole algebra
(must be assigned some value for all possible arguments),
while cycles involve few specific elements of the algebra.
Nontrivial cocycles can be evaluated on nontrivial cycles,
the result being nonzero. We start by recalling the definition of the Hochschild chain complex,

Definition 3.3.1
.

Let
A
be an associative algebra over a field, and let
M
be an
A
-bimodule. The
Hochschild chain complex

C_{n}(A,M)
is defined by
C_{n}(A,M):=M\otimes A^{\otimes n}
, with boundary map
\partial:C_{n}(A,M)\to C_{n-1}(A,M)
given by

\displaystyle\partial(m\otimes a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n})=

\displaystyle m\lhd a_{1}\otimes a_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{i}m\otimes a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{i}a_{i+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n}

\displaystyle+(-1)^{n}a_{n}\rhd m\otimes a_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n-1}.

for
a_{1}\dots a_{n}\in A
and
m\in M
and
\lhd,\,\,\rhd
are the left and right action. An element
c\in C_{n}(A,M)
is called a
Hochschild cycle
if it satisfies
\partial(c)=0
.
10
10

10

Now in our case we take
A=\mathfrak{hs}_{2}
and
M=\mathfrak{hs}_{2}^{\pi}
called twisted adjoint module. This means that the
m\lhd a=ma
while the right multiplication is
twisted
as
a\rhd m=\pi(a)m
. In our case,
\pi
is implemented as
\pi(P)=-P
and
\pi(L_{a,b})=L_{a,b}
.

Now, in the type-A case, the Maxwell equation
probes the cycle
c_{(1)}\in M\otimes A^{\otimes 2}

[
197
, App. B]

c_{(1)}=L_{ab}\otimes P^{a}\otimes P^{b}+\tfrac{1}{4}\,(\mathbf{1}\otimes L_{ab}\otimes L^{ab})-\tfrac{1}{4}\,C_{L}(\mathbf{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}\otimes\mathbf{1})\,,\hskip 20.00003ptC_{L}=-\tfrac{d(d-2)}{4}\,,

(3.3.21)

which is closed by virtue of the fact that

\{L_{ab},P^{b}\}\sim 0

and
-\tfrac{1}{2}\,L_{ab}L^{ab}\sim C_{L}\,\mathbf{1}

due to the quotient by the Joseph ideal. Note that we have taken the module
M
to be the algebra
A=\mathfrak{hs}
itself.
As it turns out, one can find a counterpart of this cycle
in
\mathfrak{hs}_{\ell}
, namely

c_{(\ell)}=\mathcal{M}_{a,b}\otimes P^{a}\otimes P^{b}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(\mathbf{1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{a,b}\otimes L^{ab}-P^{a}\otimes P^{b}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{a,b}-P^{a}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{a,b}\otimes P^{b}\big)+\dots\,,

(3.3.22)

which is closed as a consequence of the fact that

\{\mathcal{M}_{a,b},P^{b}\}\sim 0
,
up to a term

\mathbf{1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{a,b}L^{ab}
(hence the dots).
To verify that this is indeed a cycle,
first note that

\partial(\mathcal{M}_{a,b}\otimes P^{a}\otimes P^{b})=\{\mathcal{M}_{a,b},P^{a}\}\otimes P^{b}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{M}_{a,b}\otimes L^{ab}\sim-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathcal{M}_{a,b}\otimes L^{ab}\,,

(3.3.23)

as we have previously argued that
\{\mathcal{M}_{a,b},P^{a}\}

belongs to the defining ideal of
\mathfrak{hs}_{\ell}

(in fact, any higher spin algebra
containing a massless spin-
1
fields
in its spectrum), and hence is modded out.
The remaining term is compensated thanks to

\partial(\mathbf{1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{a,b}\otimes L^{ab})=\mathcal{M}_{a,b}\otimes L^{ab}-\mathbf{1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{a,b}\,L^{ab}+L^{ab}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{a,b}\,,

(3.3.24)

which however brings in two other terms. The last one
can be cancelled using

\partial(P^{a}\otimes P^{b}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{a,b}+P^{a}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{a,b}\otimes P^{b})\sim L^{ab}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{a,b}\,,

(3.3.25)

where we made use of
\{\mathcal{M}_{a,b},P^{b}\}\sim 0
again,
which leaves us with the final task of eliminating
the term proportional to
\mathbf{1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{a,b}\,L^{ab}
.
In the type-A example, we could take advantage
of the fact that the term
L_{ab}L^{ab}
is proportional
to the identity in
\mathfrak{hs}
. This is a simple consequence
of quotienting
\mathcal{U}\big(\mathfrak{so}(2,d)\big)

by the Joseph ideal.
11
11

11

More precisely,
the scalar component of the generator
\mathcal{J}_{AB}
,
when decomposed under the Lorentz algebra, relates
P^{2}

to the quadratic Casimir operator of
\mathfrak{so}(2,d)

which is itself proportional to the identity.
Since
\mathcal{C}_{2}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,L_{ab}L^{ab}+P^{2}
,
one therefore concludes that
-\tfrac{1}{2}\,L_{ab}L^{ab}

is also proportional to the identity.

We can expect that a similar property also holds
for type-A
ℓ
algebras, by inspecting its spectrum:
since
\mathcal{M}_{a,b}=L_{ab}P^{2(\ell-1)}+(\dots)

belongs to the
\mathfrak{so}(2,d)
-irrep
(2\ell-1,1)
,
the contraction
\mathcal{M}_{a,b}\,L^{ab}

belongs to
(2\ell)
,

\mathcal{M}_{a,b}\in\hbox to78pt{\vbox to23.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-11.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -15.22 M 0 -15.22 L 0 0 L 15.22 0 L 15.22 -15.22 Z M 15.22 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 20.00003pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{M}_{a,b}\,L^{ab}\in\hbox to89pt{\vbox to13.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.5pt\lower-1.30522pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 15.22 L 121.77 15.22 L 121.77 0 Z M 121.77 15.22}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{36.91664pt}{2.02779pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\subset\mathcal{I}_{\ell}\,,

(3.3.26)

since the latter is the only
\mathfrak{so}(2,d)
diagram
susceptible to contain a Lorentz scalar.
In other words,
\mathcal{M}_{a,b}\,L^{ab}

is related to the scalar part of the generator

\mathcal{J}_{\mathsf{A(2\ell)}}
, whose structure
is discussed in Appendix
A
.
We can expect that
\mathcal{M}_{a,b}\,L^{ab}

is proportional to
P^{2(\ell-1)}
, or a polynomial
in
P^{2}
of degree
\ell-1
more generally.

This is indeed the case for the type-A
2
algebra,
where
\mathcal{M}_{a,b}\,L^{ab}\sim\#\,P^{2}
,
as we show in Appendix
A
. We can therefore
use this identity and compensate the term

\mathbf{1}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{a,b}\,L^{ab}

by adding

\mathbf{1}\otimes P_{a}\otimes P^{a}\hskip 20.00003pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 20.00003pt\partial\big(\mathbf{1}\otimes P_{a}\otimes P^{a}\big)=-\mathbf{1}\otimes P^{2}\,,

(3.3.27)

which, when added with the proper coefficient
to
c_{(2)}
above, defines a cycle of
\mathfrak{hs}_{2}
.

Oscillator realisation for type-A
2
.

Let us compute the Maxwell generator
in our oscillator realisation. To do so,
first recall that the Lorentz generators
are embedded in
\mathfrak{sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
as

L^{\alpha\beta}=\tfrac{1}{4}\,\{\hat{y}^{\alpha},\hat{y}^{\beta}\}-\tfrac{1}{4}\,\epsilon^{ij}\,[\hat{\phi}^{\alpha}_{i},\hat{\phi}^{\beta}_{j}]\,,\hskip 20.00003ptL^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}=\tfrac{1}{4}\,\{\hat{y}^{\alpha^{\prime}},\hat{y}^{\beta^{\prime}}\}-\tfrac{1}{4}\,\epsilon^{ij}\,[\hat{\phi}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{i},\hat{\phi}^{\beta^{\prime}}_{j}]\,,

(3.3.28)

where we split the oscillators (
3.1.45a
)
as
\hat{Y}^{A}=(\hat{y}^{\alpha},\hat{y}^{\alpha^{\prime}})

with
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\{1,2\}
indices
for two-components spinors. Similarly,
the transvection generators read

P^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}=\tfrac{1}{4}\,\{\hat{y}^{\alpha},\hat{y}^{\alpha^{\prime}}\}-\tfrac{1}{4}\,\epsilon^{ij}\,[\hat{\phi}^{\alpha}_{i},\hat{\phi}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{j}]\,.

(3.3.29)

Let us also introduce the notation

q_{i}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\hat{y}_{\alpha}\,\hat{\phi}^{\alpha}_{i}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\bar{q}_{i}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\hat{y}_{\alpha^{\prime}}\,\hat{\phi}^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{i}\,,\hskip 20.00003ptt_{ij}=\tfrac{1}{4}\,\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\,\big[\hat{\phi}_{i}^{\alpha},\hat{\phi}_{j}^{\beta}\big]\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\bar{t}_{ij}=\tfrac{1}{4}\,\epsilon_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}\,\big[\hat{\phi}_{i}^{\alpha^{\prime}},\hat{\phi}_{j}^{\beta^{\prime}}\big]\,,

(3.3.30)

in terms of which the
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})
generators read

Q_{i}=q_{i}+\bar{q}_{i}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\tau_{ij}=t_{ij}+\bar{t}_{ij}\,.

(3.3.31)

Note that
q_{i}
and
t_{ij}
form an
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})

algebra, and
\bar{q}_{i}
and
\bar{t}_{ij}
as well.
The square of the translation generators
can be written as

P^{2}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,P_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\,P^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}=\tfrac{2p-1}{2}+q_{i}\,\bar{q}^{i}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,t_{ij}\,\bar{t}^{ij}\,,

(3.3.32)

where the factor
-\tfrac{1}{2}
comes from
the
\gamma
-matrices used to convert vector indices
into spinor ones.
12
12

12

This can also be check by
comparing
[L_{ab},P^{b}]
and
[P_{a},P^{2}]

with their spinor counterparts, which shows that
one should use

L_{a}\to\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}L_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}+\epsilon_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}L_{\alpha\beta}\big)
and

P_{a}\to\tfrac{i}{\sqrt{2}}\,P_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}
.

The Maxwell generator for
p=1\Leftrightarrow\ell=2

therefore becomes

\mathcal{M}_{\alpha\beta}=L_{\alpha\beta}\,\big(q_{i}\,\bar{q}^{i}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\bar{t}_{ij}\,t^{ij}+\tfrac{1}{2}\big)\,,

(3.3.33)

and similarly for
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}
,
upon exchanging
L_{\alpha\beta}
with
L_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}
.
A direct computation leads to

\big\{\mathcal{M}_{\alpha\beta},P^{\beta}{}_{\alpha^{\prime}}\big\}\sim 0\,,

(3.3.34)

upon using the identities

\hat{\phi}^{\alpha}_{i}\,t^{2}=-2\,\hat{\phi}^{\alpha}_{j}\,t_{i}{}^{j}\,,\hskip 20.00003ptt_{ik}\,t_{j}{}^{k}=-2\,t_{ij}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\epsilon_{ij}\,t^{2}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\text{and}\hskip 20.00003pt(t_{ij}+2\epsilon_{ij})\,t^{2}=6\,t_{ij}\,,

(3.3.35)

with
t^{2}\equiv t_{ij}\,t^{ij}
,
which can be proved thanks to Fierz identities.

Let us conclude this section by pointing a subtlety
in the computation of the ideal generators
in our oscillator realisation. Introducing
\hbar

in the canonical anti/commutation relations as

[\hat{Y}^{A},\hat{Y}^{B}]=2\hbar\,C^{AB}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\{\hat{\phi}^{A}_{i},\hat{\phi}^{B}_{j}\}=2\hbar\,C^{AB}\,\epsilon_{ij}\,,

(3.3.36)

the
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})
anti/commutation relations read

\{Q_{i},Q_{j}\}=\hbar\,\tau_{ij}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt[\tau_{ij},Q_{k}]=2\hbar\,\epsilon_{k(i}\,Q_{j)}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt[\tau_{ij},\tau_{kl}]=\hbar\,\big(\epsilon_{kj}\,\tau_{il}+\dots\big)\,,

(3.3.37)

i.e. the right hand side of any anti/commutator
is proportional to
\hbar
. Contracting the second
relation with
\epsilon^{jk}
yields

\hbar\,q_{i}=-\tfrac{1}{2p+1}\,[t_{ij},Q^{j}]\hskip 20.00003pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 20.00003pt\hbar^{2}\,t_{ij}=-\tfrac{1}{2p+1}\,\big\{[t_{ij},Q^{j}],q_{j}\big\}\,,

(3.3.38)

which could, in the absence of
\hbar
, lead one
to conclude that
q_{i}
and
t_{ij}
can be set to zero
(and similarly for
\bar{q}_{i}
and
\bar{t}_{ij}
),
when taking the quotient by
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})
. This would
however be incorrect since it would amount to quotienting
by
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})\oplus\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})
, one copy
generated by
q_{i}
and
t_{ij}
, and another copy by

\bar{q}_{i}
and
\bar{t}_{ij}
. This direct sum
is Howe dual to
\mathfrak{sp}(2,\mathbb{R})\oplus\mathfrak{sp}(2,\mathbb{R})
,
and not to
\mathfrak{sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
, as each copy of
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})

does not commute with the transvection generators

P_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}
. Consequently, it would be inconsistent
to mod out
q_{i}
and
\bar{q}_{i}
separately (and similarly
for
t_{ij}
and
\bar{t}_{ij}
) in the centraliser
of
\mathfrak{sp}(4,\mathbb{R})
— in the sense that the resulting algebra
would not be related to the type-A
ℓ
higher spin
algebra.

The introduction of
\hbar
in computation also proves
useful when it comes to checking that the scalar generator
of the ideal also vanishes: the expression (
3.3.32
)
of
P^{2}
can be re-written as

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{2}\,P_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\,P^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}=\hbar^{2}\,\tfrac{2p-1}{2}+q_{i}\,\bar{q}^{i}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,t_{ij}\,\bar{t}^{ij}=\hbar^{2}\,\tfrac{2p-1}{2}+q_{i}\,(Q^{i}-q^{i})+\tfrac{1}{2}\,t_{ij}\,(\tau^{ij}-t^{ij})\,,

(3.3.39)

Evaluating
\mathcal{J}_{\bullet}^{(2)}
, which involves
the previous equation for
p=1
and modulo
Q_{i}

and
\tau_{ij}
, yields

\mathcal{J}_{\bullet}^{(2)}=\big(P^{2}-\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\big)\,\big(P^{2}-\tfrac{5\hbar^{2}}{2}\big)\sim\big(q_{i}\,q^{i}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,t_{ij}\,t^{ij}\big)\,\big(q_{k}\,q^{k}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,t_{kl}\,t^{kl}+2\,\hbar^{2}\big)\sim\big(q_{i}\,q^{i}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,t_{ij}\,t^{ij}\big)^{2}\,,

(3.3.40)

upon using
\hbar\,q_{i}\sim 0\sim\hbar^{2}\,t_{ij}
.
Using again Fierz identity and (
3.3.35
),
one can show that

\big(q_{i}\,q^{i}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,t_{ij}\,t^{ij}\big)^{2}\sim 0\hskip 20.00003pt\text{i.e.}\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{J}_{\bullet}^{(2)}\sim 0\,,

(3.3.41)

modulo
Q_{i}
and
\tau_{ij}
, as required.

3.4
Discussion

In this chapter, we proposed a new realisation
of the type-A
ℓ
higher spin algebra
in four dimensions, based on extending
the Weyl algebra with a Clifford algebra.
This allows for an arguably simpler realisation
of
\mathfrak{hs}_{\ell}
, wherein the limit of the range
of values of the depth of the partially massless fields
is constrained by the dimension of the Clifford algebra.
We also exhibited a Hochschild
3
-cycle
of
\mathfrak{hs}_{\ell}
, which suggests that there should exist
non-trivial deformations of the partially massless
higher spin algebras.

Unfortunately, the usual technique used
to construct deformation of higher spin algebra
that consists in using deformed oscillators
[
217
]
,
i.e. trading
\hat{Y}^{A}
for
\hat{q}^{A}

which satisfy

[\hat{q}^{A},\hat{q}^{B}]=2\,C^{AB}\,(\mathbf{1}+k\,\nu\big)\,,

(3.4.1)

where
k
is the generator of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}

action on the Weyl algebra,
discussed in the previous section, does
not

seem to work: we were unable to use this deformation
and preserve a realisation of the
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})
algebra

undeformed
. Indeed, note first that,
assuming that the deformed oscillators
\hat{q}^{A}

still
commute
with the fermionic oscillators
implies

0=\big[\phi^{A}_{i},[\hat{q}^{B},\hat{q}^{C}]\big]=2\nu\,C^{BC}\,[\phi^{A}_{i},k]\hskip 20.00003pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 20.00003pt[\phi^{A}_{i},k]=0\,,

(3.4.2)

i.e. the fermionic oscillators should also
commute

with the generator of the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
action—also called the Klein operator.
A direct computation yields

\{Q_{i},Q_{j}\}=(\mathbf{1}+k\,\nu)\,\tau_{ij}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\text{with}\hskip 20.00003ptQ_{i}\equiv\tfrac{1}{2}\,C_{AB}\,\hat{q}^{A}\,\hat{\phi}^{B}_{i}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\tau_{ij}=\tfrac{1}{4}\,C_{AB}\,[\hat{\phi}^{A}_{i},\hat{\phi}^{B}_{j}]\,,

(3.4.3)

which deforms the
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})
algebra.
One could think of modifying the odd generators as

Q_{i}^{\rm new}=f(k,\nu)\,Q_{i}\,,\hskip 20.00003ptf(k,\nu)=a(\nu)\,\mathbf{1}+b(\nu)\,k\,,\hskip 20.00003pta(0)=1\,,\hskip 10.00002ptb(0)=0\,,

(3.4.4)

however, this leads to

\{Q_{i}^{\rm new},Q_{j}^{\rm new}\}=f(k,\nu)f(-k,\nu)\,(\mathbf{1}+k\,\nu)\,\tau_{ij}\,,

(3.4.5)

which does not allow us to remove the factor

\mathbf{1}+k\nu
by suitably choosing
f(k,\nu)
,
since the combination that appears,

f(k,\nu)f(-k,\nu)=\big(a(\nu)^{2}-b(\nu)^{2}\big)\mathbf{1}
,
as it is only proportional to the identity.
If one could require that the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
generator
anticommute with the fermionic oscillators,

\{k,\phi^{A}_{i}\}=0
, the right hand side
of the above equation could be fixed to be
the (undeformed) generators of the
\mathfrak{sp}(2p,\mathbb{R})

subalgebra
\tau_{ij}
by suitably choosing
f(k,\nu)
,
thereby providing us with a realisation of
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})

in the deformed oscillator algebra. Unfortunately,
we saw that requiring the Klein operator and the fermionic
oscillators to anticommute is inconsistent.

This situation seems surprising
since in the case of the type-B algebra, whose realisation
is also based on a quotient of the Weyl–Clifford algebra

[
218
]
, and are known to admit deformations
of this type
[
205
,
206
]
.
The deformed oscillator algebra, which first appeared
in a paper of Wigner
[
219
]
,
is one of the simplest example
of a
symplectic reflection algebra

(originally introduced by Etingof and Ginzburg

[
220
]
, see also

[
221
,
222
,
223
]

for more recent reviews). The algebras are deformations
of the smash product of the Weyl algebra
with a finite group (acting on it by automorphisms).
The latter naturally contains reductive dual pairs

(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
of bosonic type,
which can — at least in some cases

[
224
,
225
,
226
]
—
be deformed by finding
a realisation of one of the algebra of the pair,
say
\mathfrak{g}
, in a symplectic reflection algebra.
Typically, the other algebra
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}

is deformed to an
associative
(not Lie) algebra.
In any case, both algebras are mutual centralisers
of one another, and hence one again finds a bijection
between their representations (appearing in the appropriate
Fock space). Recently, some examples of dual pairs
of Lie
superalgebras
have been deformed

[
227
,
228
]
using symplectic
reflection algebras. The difference with respect to
the pair
\big(\mathfrak{sp}(2n,\mathbb{R}),\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})\big)
of interest
for us is that the superalgebra
\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})

that we would like to preserve when using the deformed
oscillator has its bosonic subalgebra
\mathfrak{sp}(2p,\mathbb{R})

realised using only fermionic oscillators
which are not deformed (since they generate
a Clifford algebra which is finite-dimensional,
it does not admit a non-trivial deformation).
This seems to be one of the reasons why preserving

\mathfrak{osp}(1|2p,\mathbb{R})
appears impossible, at least
if we simply replace the bosonic oscillators
\hat{Y}^{A}

by deformed ones
\hat{q}^{A}
in our realisation.

A
More on the Type-A
2
Algebra

Any higher spin algebra whose spectrum consists of
totally symmetric fields
only
, and defined as
a quotient of
\mathcal{U}\big(\mathfrak{so}(2,d)\big)

by an ideal
\mathcal{I}
, will necessarily contain
the antisymmetric generator
13
13

13

The factor

4
in
V_{\mathsf{ABCD}}
has been added for simplicity.

V_{\mathsf{ABCD}}=4\,M_{\mathsf{[AB}}\,M_{\mathsf{CD]}}=M_{\mathsf{[AB}}\,M_{\mathsf{C]D}}-M_{\mathsf{[AB}}\,\eta_{\mathsf{C]D}}\,,

(A.1)

in its defining ideal
\mathcal{I}
.
We will therefore start this appendix
by reviewing how factoring out
V_{\mathsf{ABDC}}

relates all Casimir operators to the quadratic one
(see also
[
203
, Sec. 2.1]

and
[
229
]
). Let us illustrate
this mechanism in the case of the quartic
Casimir operator, defined as
14
14

14

More generally, we follow the convention
that the Casimir operator of
\mathfrak{so}(2,d)

of order
2n
is given by

\mathcal{C}_{2n}:=\tfrac{1}{2}\,M_{\mathsf{A_{1}}}{}^{\mathsf{A_{2}}}\,M_{\mathsf{A_{2}}}{}^{\mathsf{A_{3}}}\,\dots\,M_{\mathsf{A_{2n}}}{}^{\mathsf{A_{1}}}
.

\mathcal{C}_{4}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,M_{\mathsf{A}}{}^{\mathsf{B}}\,M_{\mathsf{B}}{}^{\mathsf{C}}\,M_{\mathsf{C}}{}^{\mathsf{D}}\,M_{\mathsf{D}}{}^{\mathsf{A}}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{C}_{2}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,M_{\mathsf{AB}}\,M^{\mathsf{AB}}\,.

(A.2)

A direct computation yields
15
15

15

For all computations in this appendix,
one needs to use a few identities
that are specific to orthogonal algebra,
which we will list here. For
so_{N}
,
with generators
R_{IJ}=-R_{JI}
obeying

[R_{IJ},R_{KL}]=\eta_{JK}\,R_{IL}+(\dots)

with
\eta
of
arbitrary signature
, one has

[R_{I}{}^{\bullet},R_{J\bullet}]=-(N-2)\,R_{IJ}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt[V^{\bullet},R_{I\bullet}]=-(N-1)\,V_{I}\,,\hskip 20.00003ptR_{I}{}^{J}\,R_{J}{}^{K}\,R_{K}{}^{I}=-\tfrac{N-2}{2}\,R_{IJ}\,R^{IJ}\,,

where
V_{I}
is any vector of
so_{N}
.

V_{\mathsf{ABC}}{}^{\bullet}\,M_{\mathsf{D\bullet}}=M_{\mathsf{AB}}\,M_{\mathsf{C}}{}^{\bullet}\,M_{\mathsf{D\bullet}}+2\,M_{\mathsf{C[A}}\,M_{\mathsf{D}}{}^{\bullet}\,M_{\mathsf{B]\bullet}}+M_{\mathsf{AB}}\,M_{\mathsf{CD}}-2\,(d-1)\,M_{\mathsf{C[A}}\,M_{\mathsf{B]D}}\,,

(A.3)

which, upon taking a trace in
\mathsf{CD}

and contracting with
M_{\mathsf{AB}}
, gives

V_{\mathsf{ABCD}}\sim 0\hskip 20.00003pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{C}_{4}\sim\mathcal{C}_{2}\,\big(\mathcal{C}_{2}+\tfrac{d(d-1)}{2}\big)\,,

(A.4)

in agreement with
[
203
, Sec. 2.1]

in the special case of the singleton,
and with
[
230
]
in general.
Similarly, taking the Lorentz components
V_{abcd}
,
and contracting them with
L^{ab}
(on the left)
and
L^{cd}
(on the right), one finds

V_{abcd}\sim 0\hskip 20.00003pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{C}_{4}^{L}\sim\big(\mathcal{C}_{2}-P^{2}\big)\Big(\mathcal{C}_{2}-P^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,(d-1)(d-2)\Big)\,,

(A.5)

where
\mathcal{C}_{4}^{L}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,L_{a}{}^{b}\,L_{b}{}^{c}\,L_{c}{}^{d}\,L_{d}{}^{a}
is the quartic Casimir operator
of the Lorentz subalgebra. This is the same type
of relation as (
A.4
) with
d\to d-1
,
upon using the fact the quadratic Casimir operator

\mathcal{C}_{2}^{L}
of
\mathfrak{so}(1,d)

is given in terms of that of
\mathfrak{so}(2,d)
by

\mathcal{C}_{2}^{L}=\mathcal{C}_{2}-P^{2}
.
Contracting
V_{\mathsf{ABCD}}
with more generators
produces similar identities,
relating Casimir operators of order
2n

to lower order ones, and ultimately to
\mathcal{C}_{2}
.

When decomposing the generator
V_{\mathsf{ABCD}}

under
\mathfrak{so}(1,d)
, one finds an additional antisymmetric
generator of rank
3
, namely
V_{abc0^{\prime}}
.
Contracting it with
L^{ab}
(on the left)
and
P^{c}
(on the right) yields the identity

V_{abc0^{\prime}}\sim 0\hskip 20.00003pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 20.00003ptL_{a}{}^{\bullet}\,L_{b\bullet}\,\{P^{a},P^{b}\}\sim-2\,\big(\mathcal{C}_{2}-P^{2}\big)\,\Big(P^{2}+\tfrac{d-1}{2}\big)\,,

(A.6)

which will be useful for us later on.

Type-A
2
.

Let us define

W_{\mathsf{AB}}:=M_{\mathsf{(A}}{}^{\mathsf{C}}\,M_{\mathsf{B)C}}\,,

(A.7)

and consider the symmetric generator for the ideal
defining the partially massless higher spin
algebra
A_{2}
, which is the traceless part
of
W^{\mathsf{(AB}}\,W^{\mathsf{CD)}}
, given by,

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\mathsf{ABCD}}

\displaystyle:=W_{\mathsf{(AB}}\,W_{\mathsf{CD)}}-\tfrac{4}{d+6}\,\eta_{\mathsf{(AB}}\,\big(W_{\mathsf{C}}{}^{\mathsf{M}}\,W_{\mathsf{D)M}}-\mathcal{C}_{2}\,W_{\mathsf{CD)}}\big)

(A.8)

\displaystyle+\tfrac{4}{(d+4)(d+6)}\,\eta_{\mathsf{(AB}}\,\eta_{\mathsf{CD)}}\,\big(\mathcal{C}_{4}+\mathcal{C}_{2}\,[\mathcal{C}_{2}-(\tfrac{d}{2})^{2}]\big)\,,

where we used the relation

\tfrac{1}{2}\,W_{\mathsf{AB}}\,W^{\mathsf{AB}}=\mathcal{C}_{4}-(\tfrac{d}{2})^{2}\,\mathcal{C}_{2}\,,

(A.9)

relating the contraction of the generator
W_{AB}

with itself and the quadratic and quartic Casimir
operators. Note that we can also express
this generator of the ideal
\mathcal{I}_{2}
as

\mathcal{J}_{\mathsf{ABCD}}=\mathcal{J}_{\mathsf{(AB}}\,\mathcal{J}_{\mathsf{CD)}}-\tfrac{4}{d+6}\,\eta_{\mathsf{(AB}}\,\mathcal{J}_{\mathsf{C}}{}^{\bullet}\,\mathcal{J}_{\mathsf{D)\bullet}}+\tfrac{4}{(d+4)(d+6)}\,\eta_{\mathsf{(AB}}\eta_{\mathsf{CD)}}\,\big(\mathcal{C}_{4}-\tfrac{2}{d+2}\,\mathcal{C}_{2}^{2}\,-(\tfrac{d}{2})^{2}\,\mathcal{C}_{2}\big)\,,

(A.10)

where
