according to which the action obtained from
f

is the sum of the conformally-coupled scalar
and a Noether coupling part,

S_{\scriptstyle\rm Noether}[h,\phi]=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\Tr_{A}(F_{hs}\ast\mathcal{W}_{\phi})=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\int_{\mathscr{X}}\mathrm{d}^{D}x\,|e|\,\phi^{*}\,\big[\rho(F_{hs})\Phi\big]\rvert_{y=0}\,,

(4.5.22)

corresponding to the contribution of the higher spin
currents coupled to higher spin sources/ background fields

h^{a_{1}\dots a_{s}}
. In other words, we can identify
the higher spin current by putting the above functional
in the form

S_{\scriptstyle\rm Noether}[h,\phi]=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\int_{\mathscr{X}}\mathrm{d}^{D}x\,|e|\ \sum_{s>2}h^{a_{1}\dots a_{s}}\,J_{a_{1}\dots a_{s}}(\phi)\,,

(4.5.23)

where the spin
s
current
J_{a_{1}\dots a_{s}}

here is by definition bilinear in the scalar field
\phi
.

This computation involves the action of the quantization map
on the lift of
f_{hs}
, which is of arbitrary order in
p
.
As a consequence, the relevant terms to compute in this lift,
meaning those that will contribute to the final result
after applying the quantization of
F_{hs}
to
\Phi

and setting
y=0
, are those that are
y
-independent
or contain
exactly
the same number of
y
’s and
p
’s.
Indeed, the quantization map applied to a monomial
of order
l
in
y
and
m
in
p
reads

\rho(y^{a_{1}}\dots y^{a_{l}}\,p_{b_{1}}\dots p_{b_{m}})=(-\hbar)^{m}\sum_{k=0}^{\min(l,m)}\tfrac{1}{2^{k}}\,\tfrac{m!}{(m-k)!}\tfrac{l!}{k!(l-k)!}y^{(a_{1}}\dots y^{a_{l-k}}\delta^{a_{l+1-k}}_{(b_{1}}\dots\delta^{a_{l})}_{b_{k}}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y^{b_{k+1}}}\dots\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y^{b_{m})}}\,,

(4.5.24)

so that when setting
y=0
, only monomials with
l\leq m
,
i.e. less
y
’s than
p
’s, remain. This would be difficult
to compute for arbitrary spin
s>2
, so we will focus
on the curvature independent part of the current.
The relevant part of the lift of
f_{hs}

is therefore given by its ‘covariant Taylor series’,

F_{hs}=\sum_{k\geq 0}\tfrac{1}{k!}\,y^{a_{1}}\dots y^{a_{k}}\,\nabla_{a_{1}}\dots\nabla_{a_{k}}f_{hs}+\dots\,,

(4.5.25)

where the dots denote curvature corrections.
Applying the quantization map on this (partial) lift,
and evaluating the result at
y=0
, one ends up with

\displaystyle\rho(F_{hs})\rvert_{y=0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s>2}(-\hbar)^{s}\,\sum_{k=0}^{s}\tfrac{1}{2^{k}}\tfrac{s!}{k!(s-k)!}\,\nabla_{a_{1}}\dots\nabla_{a_{k}}h^{a_{1}\dots a_{k}\,a_{k+1}\dots a_{s}}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y^{a_{k+1}}}\dots\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y^{a_{s}}}+\dots\,.

(4.5.26)

Under the same restrictions, the lift of the scalar field reads

\Phi=\sum_{k\geq 0}\tfrac{1}{k!}\,y^{a_{1}}\dots y^{a_{k}}\,\nabla_{a_{1}}\dots\nabla_{a_{k}}\phi+\dots\,,

(4.5.27)

so that,

\displaystyle\rho(F_{hs})\Phi\rvert_{y=0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s>2}(-\hbar)^{s}\,\sum_{k=0}^{s}\tfrac{1}{2^{k}}\tfrac{s!}{k!(s-k)!}\,\nabla_{a_{1}}\dots\nabla_{a_{k}}h^{a_{1}\dots a_{k}\,a_{k+1}\dots a_{s}}\nabla_{a_{k+1}}\dots\nabla_{a_{s}}\phi+\dots\,,

(4.5.28)

again keeping only curvature independent terms.
Upon integration by parts, one finds

\displaystyle J_{a_{1}\dots a_{s}}

\displaystyle=\big(-\tfrac{\hbar}{2}\big)^{s}\,\sum_{k=0}^{s}\tfrac{(-1)^{k}\,s!}{k!(s-k)!}\,\nabla_{(a_{1}}\dots\nabla_{a_{k}}\phi^{*}\,\nabla_{a_{k+1}}\dots\nabla_{a_{s)}}\phi+\dots\,,

(4.5.29)

as one may have expected. This is the covariantized version
of the well-known ‘dipole’ generating function

\phi^{*}(x-y)\phi(x+y)
that yields conserved quasi-primary
(higher spin) currents with an admixture of descendants
in the flat space. The curvature corrections
can systematically be worked out, see
[
83
]

for the spin-three example in the bottom-up approach.
However, it is clear that the higher the spin
the more non-linearities in the Riemann tensor
R

and its derivatives will enter. Therefore, eq. (
4.4.19
)
seems to be the most compact way of writing the coupling
of the free scalar field to a higher spin background.

First order correction in curvature.

If we focus on the spin-
3
case, then we only need
to compute the lift of
f_{s=3}=h^{abc}\,p_{a}p_{b}p_{c}

to order
3
. Pushing the computation of the lift
of any symbol
f(x,p)
presented in (
4.3.23
)
to the next order, thanks to the recursion (
4.3.22
),
yields

\displaystyle\tau(f)

\displaystyle=\Big(1+y^{a}\nabla_{a}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,y^{a}y^{b}\,\big[\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}+\tfrac{1}{3}\,R_{da}{}^{c}{}_{b}\,p_{c}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial p_{d}}\big]

(4.5.30)

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt+\tfrac{1}{6}\,y^{a}y^{b}y^{c}\,\big[\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\nabla_{c}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\nabla_{a}R_{db}{}^{e}{}_{c}\,p_{e}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial p_{d}}+R_{da}{}^{e}{}_{b}\,p_{e}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial p_{d}}\,\nabla_{c}\big]

(4.5.31)

\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt+\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{12}\,y^{a}\,\big[R_{ab}{}^{d}{}_{c}\,\tfrac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{b}\partial p_{c}}\nabla_{d}-\tfrac{1}{4}\,\nabla_{b}R_{ac}{}^{e}{}_{d}\,p_{e}\,\tfrac{\partial^{3}}{\partial p_{b}\partial p_{c}\partial p_{d}}\big]+\dots\Big)\,f\,,

(4.5.32)

and applying it to
f_{s=3}
, one finds that

F_{s=3}=\tau(f_{s=3})
is given by

\displaystyle F_{s=3}

\displaystyle=\Big(h^{abc}+y^{i}\,\nabla_{i}h^{abc}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,y^{i}y^{j}\,\big[\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}h^{abc}+R_{di}{}^{a}{}_{j}h^{bcd}\big]

(4.5.33)

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt+\tfrac{1}{6}\,y^{i}y^{j}y^{k}\,\big[\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\nabla_{k}h^{abc}+\tfrac{3}{2}\,\nabla_{i}R_{dj}{}^{a}{}_{k}\,h^{bcd}+3\,R_{di}{}^{a}{}_{j}\,\nabla_{k}h^{bcd}\big]+\dots\Big)\,p_{a}p_{b}p_{c}

(4.5.34)

\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt+\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\,y^{i}\,\big[R_{ib}{}^{d}{}_{c}\,\nabla_{d}h^{abc}-\tfrac{1}{4}\,\nabla_{b}R_{ic}{}^{a}{}_{d}\,h^{bcd}\big]\,p_{a}+\dots\,,

(4.5.35)

where the dots denote terms of order
4
or higher,
and its quantization evaluated at
y=0
reads

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{\hbar^{3}}\,\rho(F_{s=3})\rvert_{y=0}

\displaystyle=h^{abc}\,\tfrac{\partial^{3}}{\partial y^{a}\partial y^{b}\partial y^{c}}+\tfrac{3}{2}\,\nabla_{a}h^{abc}\,\tfrac{\partial^{2}}{\partial y^{b}\partial y^{c}}+\tfrac{1}{4}\,\big[3\,\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}h^{abc}-R_{ab}\,h^{abc}\big]\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y^{c}}

(4.5.36)

\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt+\tfrac{1}{8}\,\big[\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\nabla_{c}h^{abc}-\nabla_{a}R_{bc}\,h^{abc}-R_{ab}\,\nabla_{c}h^{abc}\big]\,.

(4.5.37)

Similarly, we can use (
4.3.42
) to compute
the lift of the scalar field
\phi
to order
3
,

\Phi=\phi+y^{a}\nabla_{a}\phi+\tfrac{1}{2}\,y^{a}y^{b}\,\big(\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}-\tfrac{1}{6}\,R_{ab}\big)\phi+\tfrac{1}{6}\,y^{a}y^{b}y^{c}\,\big(\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\nabla_{c}-\tfrac{1}{4}\,\nabla_{a}R_{bc}-\tfrac{1}{2}\,R_{ab}\,\nabla_{c}\big)\,\phi+\dots\,,

(4.5.38)

so that one finds

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{\hbar^{3}}\,\rho(F_{s=3})\Phi\rvert_{y=0}

\displaystyle=h^{abc}\,\big(\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\nabla_{c}-\tfrac{3}{8}\,\nabla_{a}R_{bc}-\tfrac{3}{4}\,R_{ab}\,\nabla_{c}\big)\,\phi+\tfrac{3}{2}\,\nabla_{c}h^{abc}\,\big(\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}-\tfrac{1}{4}\,R_{ab}\big)\,\phi

(4.5.39)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\tfrac{3}{4}\,\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}h^{abc}\,\nabla_{c}\phi+\tfrac{1}{8}\,\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\nabla_{c}h^{abc}\,\phi\,.

which leads to the following expression

\displaystyle J_{abc}

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{\hbar^{3}}{8}\,\Big(\nabla_{(a}\nabla_{b}\nabla_{c)}\phi\,\phi^{*}-3\,\nabla_{(a}\nabla_{b}\phi\,\nabla_{c)}\phi^{*}+3\,\nabla_{(a}\phi\,\nabla_{b}\nabla_{c)}\phi^{*}-\phi\,\nabla_{(a}\nabla_{b}\nabla_{c)}\phi^{*}

(4.5.40)

\displaystyle\hskip 250.0pt-3\,R_{(ab}\,\nabla_{c)}\phi\,\phi^{*}+3\,R_{(ab}\,\phi\,\nabla_{c)}\phi^{*}\Big)\,,

(4.5.41)

for the spin-
3
current. More generally, the currents
up to first order in the curvature tensor (and its derivatives)
are obtained from the generating function

{\cal J}(x|u)=e^{-\frac{\hbar}{2}\,u\cdot[\nabla_{1}-\nabla_{2}]}\,\Big(1-\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{8}\,{\rm sinhc}(\tfrac{\hbar}{4}\,u\cdot\nabla_{3})\,R_{ab}(x_{3})\,u^{a}u^{b}+{\cal O}(R^{2})\Big)\,\phi(x_{1})\,\phi^{*}(x_{2})\big|_{x_{i}=x}\,,

(4.5.42)

where

{\cal J}(x|u):=\sum_{s\geq 0}\tfrac{(-\hbar)^{s}}{2^{s}\,s!}\,J_{a_{1}\dots a_{s}}(x)\,u^{a_{1}}\dots u^{a_{s}}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt{\rm sinhc}(z):=\frac{\sinh(z)}{z}\,,

(4.5.43)

and
\nabla_{i}
denotes the covariant derivative
with respect to
x_{i}
(see Appendix
D

for the derivation of this formula).
Let us remark here that, in order to make contact
with, say the computation of
[
83
]

for the spin-three current or even the standard computation
of the energy-momentum tensor, one should find the correct field
redefinition bringing the components of monomials in
p

into the appropriate field frame (combination of conformal higher spin fields and derivatives thereof),
see e.g.
[
76
, App. F]
and
[
77
]

for an instance of the same issue around a flat background.

4.6
Discussion and Summary

The work presented in this chapter is a natural continuation of the quest to covariantize the construction of conformal higher spin
gravities started in
[
78
]
.
Now, both the action for conformal higher spin gravity

S_{CHS}[h_{s}]
and the coupling of the scalar matter
to the higher spin background,

\bra{\Phi}\widehat{H}[\phi_{s}]\ket{\Phi}
,
can be written in a covariant way. The result completes
the study initiated in
[
83
]
,
where the mixing between covariant spin-three
and spin-one currents that couple to background fields
have been discussed in
D=4
. In addition, one can consider
the matter coupled conformal higher spin gravity,
see
[
253
]
for some amplitudes
in this theory over flat background. Note, however,
that while the scalar matter can be coupled
to a higher spin background for any
n
the conformal anomaly
recipe gives
S_{CHS}[h_{s}]
only for
n
even.

The results open up the possibility of considering more general
matter fields in the relevant higher spin background,
such as the higher-derivative scalar fields (also known as
higher order singletons
[
65
]
),
or spinor (and its higher-derivative counterpart),
see
[
205
]
.
The latter would in principle require the use
of the supersymmetry version of the FFS cocycle,
i.e. the representative of the cohomology class
of the Clifford–Weyl algebra dual to the unique
Hochschild homology class of the same algebra

[
254
]
.

Another possible application of the results
is to conformally-invariant differential operators.
Conformal geometry (in the sense of gauge symmetries
realized by diffeomorphisms and Weyl transformations)
is a part of the higher spin system. As we showed,
one can derive the conformal Laplacian as a particular instance
of the scalar field coupled to the conformal gravity
background. Generalizations such as Paneitz
[
255
]

or Fradkin–Tseytlin
[
256
]
operators
and GJMS operators
[
257
]
can also be recovered
by considering
F=(p^{2})^{k}+\dots

that would lead to operators of type
(\nabla^{2})^{k}+\dots
,
i.e. starting with the
k
th power of the Laplacian,
and corrected by curvature terms.

It would also be interesting to apply the deformation
quantization techniques to the self-dual conformal higher spin
gravity
[
258
,
90
]
that is natural
to formulate on twistor space. Here, the underlying space

\mathbb{CP}^{3}
is already symplectic. The twistor description
of low-spin fields,
s=1,2
, requires usual (holomorphic)
connections and vector-valued one-forms, which can be understood
as differential operators of zeroth and first order.
An extension to higher spin calls for differential operators
of arbitrary order, i.e. to the quantization
of the cotangent bundle again
(see also
[
259
]
for additional discussions
of the quantization of the cotangent in relation with
the definition of higher spin diffeomorphisms).

Let us also note that the results and techniques of this chapter bridges a gap in the phase space approach to quantum mechanics.
Indeed, one can attempt to extend the Fedosov construction
to accommodate all the usual ingredients required
in quantum mechanics. The trace is, obviously, given by
the Feigin–Felder–Shoikhet cocycle; wave functions
can be understood as covariantly constant elements
in the Fock representation obtained via the quantization map.
Wigner function takes exactly the same form as in the flat space,
but in the fiber. The basic ingredients above do not rely
on the phase space being a cotangent bundle and should extend to
arbitrary symplectic manifolds (a polarization is needed
to define the Fock space). This seems to depart
from the usual approach of symbol calculus on curved background,
e.g.
[
260
,
261
,
262
,
263
,
264
,
265
,
266
]

and references therein.

Finally, it would be interesting to construct
the
3d
matter-coupled conformal higher spin gravity,
where the ‘dynamics’ of conformal higher spin fields
is given by the Chern–Simons action
(as there is no conformal anomaly in
3d
).
Such a theory, namely the one based on fermionic matter,
can be seen to exist with the help of the argument
based on the parity anomaly
[
267
]

(see e.g.
[
268
,
269
,
270
]

for original papers on the derivation of Chern–Simons
theory from the parity anomaly and
[
271
]

for the spin-three case). An alternative idea
along the AdS/CFT correspondence lines
was recently explored in
[
272
,
273
]
.

A
A Brief Review of Weyl Calculus

Let us give a brief summary of the definition
and construction of the Wigner function in flat space
(following e.g. the textbook
[
274
]
,
or the papers
[
76
,
275
,
276
,
77
]
).

Quantization map in flat space.

The deformation quantization of
\mathbb{R}^{2D}\cong T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{D}
,
amounts to defining an isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\cal D}(\mathbb{R}^{D})\,,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

(A.1)

where
{\cal D}(\mathbb{R}^{D})
stands for the space
of differential operators on
\mathbb{R}^{D}
. This map
is referred to as a ‘quantization map’ since,
as we will recall shortly, it allows one to define
a star-product on the algebra of functions
T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{D}
,
and hence a quantization thereof. To do so,
we can take advantage of the Fourier transform
in flat space, that we denote by

({\cal F}f)(u,v):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2D}}\tfrac{{\rm d}^{D}x\,{\rm d}^{D}p}{(2\pi\hbar)^{D}}\,f(x,p)\,e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}\,(x\cdot u+p\cdot v)}\,,

(A.2)

for a symbol
f(x,p)
. Given a choice of quantization
for the phase space coordinates
x^{\mu}\to\hat{x}^{\mu}

and
p_{\mu}\to\hat{p}_{\mu}
, where hatted symbols
denote the corresponding operator, we want to associate

Schematically, we want to write something like
“
\widehat{f}(\hat{x},\hat{p})\sim f(x,p)\,\delta(x-\hat{x})\,\delta(p-\hat{p})
”,
where
f(x,p)
is the symbol of the operator
\widehat{f}
.
This sketchy formula can be given a precise sense,
using the Fourier representation of the Dirac distribution,
leading to

\widehat{f}(\hat{x},\hat{p})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2D}}\tfrac{{\rm d}^{D}u\,{\rm d}^{D}v}{(2\pi\hbar)^{D}}\,({\cal F}f)(u,v)\,e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\,(u\cdot\hat{x}+v\cdot\hat{p})}\,,

(A.3)

and which is called the
Weyl ordering
of operators.
Note that the exponential operator can be re-written as

\exp\big(\tfrac{i}{\hbar}\,(u \cdot\hat x
+ v \cdot\hat p)\big)=e^{\frac{i}{2\hbar}\,u\cdot v}\,\exp\big(\tfrac{i}{\hbar}\,u \cdot\hat x\big)\,\exp\big(\tfrac{i}{\hbar}\,v \cdot\hat p\big)\,,

(A.4)

since we assume

[\hat{x}^{\mu},\hat{p}_{\nu}]=i\hbar\,\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}
.
Choosing the usual coordinate representation,

\hat{x}^{\mu}=x^{\mu}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\hat{p}_{\mu}=-i\hbar\,\partial_{\mu}\,,

(A.5)

the action of this operator on a wave function
\varphi(x)

is given by

\displaystyle(\widehat{f}\,\varphi)(x)

\displaystyle=\int\tfrac{\mathrm{d}^{D}u\,\mathrm{d}^{D}v}{(2\pi\hbar)^{D}}\,({\cal F}f)(u,v)\,e^{\frac{i}{2\hbar}\,u\cdot v}\,e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\,u\cdot x}\,\varphi(x+v)

(A.6)

\displaystyle=\int\tfrac{\mathrm{d}^{D}u\,\mathrm{d}^{D}v}{(2\pi\hbar)^{D}}\,\tfrac{{\rm d}^{n}x^{\prime}\,{\rm d}^{n}p}{(2\pi\hbar)^{D}}\,f(x^{\prime},p)\,e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}\,p\cdot v}\,e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\,u\cdot(x-x^{\prime}+\frac{v}{2})}\,\varphi(x+v)

(A.7)

\displaystyle=\int\tfrac{\mathrm{d}^{D}v\,\mathrm{d}^{D}p}{(2\pi\hbar)^{D}}\,f(\tfrac{x+v}{2},p)\,e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\,p\cdot(x-v)}\,\varphi(v)\,,

(A.8)

where the first equation is obtained using
(
A.4
) and the action of the translation operator,
the second line is merely the definition
of the Fourier transform, and the last one is the result
of integrating over
u
, which gives a Dirac distribution,
and then evaluating it by integrating over
x^{\prime}
.
Upon Taylor expanding
f
and integrating by part,
one can put this formula into an operatorial form

(\widehat{f}\varphi)(x)=f(x,p)\,\exp\Big(\!\!-\!i\hbar\,
\tfrac{\overleftarrow{\partial}}{\partial p} \cdot\big[\tfrac 12\,\tfrac{\overleftarrow{\partial}}{\partial x}
+ \tfrac{\overrightarrow{\partial}}{\partial x}\big]\Big)\,\varphi(x)\big|_{p=0}\,.

(A.9)

The Moyal–Weyl star-product can be recovered
from the composition of the two operators associated with
two symbols via the above symbol, or quantization, map.
More precisely, it can be defined as the symbol
of the composition of the quantization of two symbols, i.e.

\widehat{f}\circ\widehat{g}=\widehat{f\star g}\,.

(A.10)

To do so, let us start by recalling
that the action of a symbol
f
given above
exhibits the
kernel
of that associated operator,
namely

(\widehat{f}\,\phi)(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\mathrm{d}^{D}q\,K_{f}(x,q)\,\phi(q)\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\text{with}\hskip 20.00003ptK_{f}(x,q):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}^{D}p}{(2\pi\hbar)^{D}}\,f(\tfrac{x+q}{2},p)\,e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\,p\cdot(x-q)}\,.

(A.11)

The symbol of the operator
\widehat{f}
can be extract back
from its kernel, via its inverse transform

f(x,p)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\mathrm{d}^{D}q\,K_{f}(x+\tfrac{1}{2}\,q,x-\tfrac{1}{2}\,q)\,e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}\,p\cdot q}\,,

(A.12)

and therefore, using this together with the fact
that the integral kernel of the composition
of two operators is given by

K_{\widehat{f}\circ\widehat{g}}(x,x^{\prime})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\mathrm{d}^{D}q\,K_{\widehat{f}\,}(x,q)\,K_{\widehat{g}\,}(q,x^{\prime})\,,

(A.13)

one ends up with

\big(f\star g\big)(x,p)=\tfrac{1}{(\pi\hbar)^{2n}}\int\mathrm{d}^{D}v_{1}\,\mathrm{d}^{D}v_{2}\,\mathrm{d}^{D}w_{1}\,\mathrm{d}^{D}w_{2}\ e^{\frac{2i}{\hbar}\,(v_{1}\cdot w_{2}-v_{2}\cdot w_{1})}\,f(x+v_{1},p+w_{1})\,g(x+v_{2},p+w_{2})\,.

(A.14)

Upon Taylor expanding the two functions
f
and
g

around
(x,p)
, and integrating by part,
one

\big(f\star g\big)(x,p)=f(x,p)\,\exp\Big(\tfrac{i\hbar}2\,
\big[\tfrac{\overleftarrow{\partial}}{\partial x}
\cdot\tfrac{\overrightarrow{\partial}}{\partial p}
-\tfrac{\overleftarrow{\partial}}{\partial p}
\cdot\tfrac{\overrightarrow{\partial}}{\partial x}\big]\Big)\,g(x,p)\,,

(A.15)

Note that the Moyal–Weyl star-product is
Hermitian
,
meaning that it satisfies

(f\star g)^{*}=g^{*}\star f^{*}\,,

(A.16)

where
(-)^{*}
denotes the complex conjugation,
i.e. the latter is an anti-involution of the Weyl algebra.

One can think of the quantization map as providing
a
representation
of the Weyl algebra: identifying
the latter as the subalgebra of
polynomial

functions on
T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{D}
, wave functions which are nothing but
functions on
\mathbb{R}^{D}
, the base of the cotangent bundle
T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{D}
,
are acted upon by the former via the quantization map.
This subspace can be thought of as a Fock space,
which carries a representation of the Weyl algebra
as can be seen from the defining relation
(
A.10
).

The integration over the cotangent bundle

T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{D}
defines a trace over the space of symbols,
at least those which are compactly supported
or vanish at infinity sufficiently fast.
Indeed, in this case one finds

\Tr(f \star g)=\int_{T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{D}}\mathrm{d}^{D}x\,\mathrm{d}^{D}p\ (f\star g)(x,p)=\int_{T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{D}}\mathrm{d}^{D}x\,\mathrm{d}^{D}p\ f(x,p)\,g(x,p)=\Tr(g \star f)\,,

(A.17)

for any symbols
f
and
g
, since all higher order terms
in the star product are total derivatives on
T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{D}
,
and hence can be ignored for the aforementioned
suitable class of symbols.

Wigner function in flat space.

Having worked out how to translate the action
and the composition of differential operators in terms
of their symbol, as well as their trace, we can now
turn our attention to the computation of matrix elements
for these operators, expressing the transition probability
from one state to another. Since the latter can be expressed as

\bra{\psi}\widehat{H}\ket{\phi}=\Tr(\widehat{H} \circ\ket\phi\!\bra\psi)\,,

(A.18)

we have everything we need to derive such quantities
using symbols, provided that we know that of the projector

\ket{\phi}\!\bra{\psi}
. In light of the relation
between the symbol of an operator and its integral kernel,
we may first focus on that of the projector.
This integral kernel is easily computed,

\big(\ket{\phi}\!\langle{\psi}\ket{\varphi}\big)(x)\overset{!}{=}\phi(x)\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\mathrm{d}^{D}q\,\psi^{*}(q)\,\varphi(q)\hskip 20.00003pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 20.00003ptK_{\ket{\phi}\!\bra{\psi}}(x,q)\equiv\phi(x)\,\psi^{*}(q)\,,

(A.19)

which leads to

W[\phi,\psi](x,p)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\mathrm{d}^{D}q\ \phi(x+\tfrac{q}{2})\,\psi^{*}(x-\tfrac{q}{2})\,e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}\,p\cdot q}\,,

(A.20)

for its symbol. It obey the following useful properties

\xi\star W[\phi,\psi]=W[\widehat{\xi}\,\phi,\psi]\,,\hskip 20.00003ptW[\phi,\psi]\star\xi^{\dagger}=W[\phi,\widehat{\xi}\,\psi]\,,

(A.21)

in accordance with the fact that it is the symbol
of the projector
\ket{\phi}\!\bra{\psi}
, and

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\mathrm{d}^{D}p\ W[\phi,\psi](x,p)=\phi(x)\,\psi^{*}(x)\,.

(A.22)

Now we can replace the right hand side of (
A.18
) with its symbol counterpart, leading to

\Tr(H \star W[\phi,\psi])=\Tr(W[\widehat{H}\phi,\psi])=\int_{T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{D}}\mathrm{d}^{D}x\,\mathrm{d}^{D}p\ W[\widehat{H}\phi,\psi]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\mathrm{d}^{D}x\ \psi^{*}(x)\,(\widehat{H}\phi)(x)\,,

(A.23)

upon using the previously listed properties of
W[\phi,\psi]
,
thereby reproducing the expected result for the quantity

\bra{\psi}\widehat{H}\ket{\phi}
from a quantum mechanical
point of view. The
Wigner function

W_{\phi}

associated with a wave function
\phi
is the symbol
of the projector
\ket{\phi}\!\bra{\phi}
, i.e.

W_{\phi}(x,p):=W[\phi,\phi](x,p)\equiv\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\mathrm{d}^{D}q\ e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}\,q\cdot p}\,\phi(x+\tfrac{q}{2})\,\phi(x-\tfrac{q}{2})\,,

(A.24)

whose integral over
p
is nothing but the probability
density defined by
\phi
.

To conclude this appendix, let us prove the identity
(
A.22
) and a small variation on it
(the intertwining property (
A.21
)
can be proved by direct computation using the integral formulae
for the star-product and the quantization map),
by expressing the Wigner function in terms of star-product.
To achieve this, recall that the star-product
of a phase factor
e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\,q\cdot p}
,
where
q
is a fixed parameter, with any symbol
f(x,p)

yields

e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\,q\cdot p}\star f(x,p)=e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\,q\cdot p}f(x+\tfrac{q}{2},p)\,,\hskip 20.00003ptf(x,p)\star e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\,q\cdot p}=e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\,q\cdot p}f(x-\tfrac{q}{2},p)\,,

(A.25)

i.e. it implements translations in
x
up to a phase.
14
14

14

To be more precise, the action of translation
on elements depending on
x

only
is generated by

e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}\,q\cdot p}\star\phi(x)\star e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}\,q\cdot p}=\phi(x+q)\,,

which can be recovered from the formulae (
A.25
).

Integrating these formulae over
q
yields

\big(f\star\delta_{p}\big)(x,p)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\mathrm{d}^{D}q\,e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}\,q\cdot p}\,f(x+\tfrac{q}{2},p)\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\big(\delta_{p}\star f\big)(x,p)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\mathrm{d}^{D}q\,e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}\,q\cdot p}\,f(x-\tfrac{q}{2},p)\,,

(A.26)

where
\delta_{p}
is the Dirac distribution
in the space of momenta
p_{a}
. With these simple identities
at hand, one finds

\displaystyle\phi\star\delta_{p}\star\psi^{*}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\mathrm{d}^{D}q\,\phi(x)\star\big[e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}\,q\cdot p}\psi^{*}(x-\tfrac{1}{2}\,q)\big]

(A.27)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\mathrm{d}^{D}q\,\big[\phi(x)\star e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}\,q\cdot p}]\,\psi^{*}(x-\tfrac{1}{2}\,q)

(A.28)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\mathrm{d}^{D}q\ \phi(x+\tfrac{q}{2})\,\psi^{*}(x-\tfrac{q}{2})\,e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}\,p\cdot q}=W[\phi,\psi]\,,

(A.29)

where to pass from the first to the second line,
one should notice that since
\phi
only depends on
x
,
its star-product with any other Weyl algebra element
will produce only derivatives with respect to
p

on the latter.

Now this expression makes it relatively easy
to evaluate the integral over momenta of the Wigner function
and its derivatives with respect to
p
. Indeed,
since the only term of this star-product that depends
on momenta is the Dirac distribution, the result is of the form

\phi\star\delta_{p}\star\psi^{*}\sim\sum_{k,l\geq 0}\partial_{x}^{k}\phi\times\partial_{p}^{k+l}\delta(p)\times\partial_{x}^{l}\psi^{*}\,,

(A.30)

so that the integral over
p
schematically reads

\int\mathrm{d}^{D}p\ W[\phi,\psi]\sim\sum_{k,l\geq 0}\int\mathrm{d}^{D}p\ \partial_{p}^{k+l}\delta(p)\times(\partial_{x}^{k}\phi\,\partial_{x}^{l}\psi^{*})\,,

(A.31)

which identically vanishes for
k+l>0
since both

\phi
and
\psi
do not depend on
p
, and yields
(
A.22
) for
k=0=l
. On top of that,
since taking partial derivative with respect to
x
or
p

commutes with the star-product, the derivatives
of the Wigner function with respect to
p
are of the form

\partial_{p}^{k}W[\phi,\phi]\sim\phi\star\partial^{k}_{p}\delta_{p}\star\psi^{*}
,
and hence the same argument shows that the integral
over the momenta identically vanishes,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\mathrm{d}^{D}p\ \tfrac{\partial^{k}}{\partial p_{a_{1}}\dots\partial p_{a_{k}}}\,W[\phi,\psi]=0\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\forall k>0\,.

(A.32)

B
More on Weyl Transformations

In this appendix, we provide more details concerning
the computation of the gauge variation of the symbol

p^{2}+\alpha\,R
. For convenience, let us introduce
the tensor

\mathcal{P}_{ab}{}^{cd}:=\delta_{(a}^{c}\delta_{b)}^{d}-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\eta_{\mathsf{AB}}\,\eta^{cd}\,,

(B.1)

with which the gauge parameter
\xi_{\text{Weyl}}
,
identified in (
4.5.9
) as the one generating
Weyl transformations for the components of
the
1
-form connection
A
, is given by

\xi_{\text{Weyl}}=-\sigma\,y\cdot p-\mathcal{P}_{bc}{}^{ad}\,y^{b}y^{c}p_{d}\,\nabla_{a}\sigma-\tfrac{1}{3}\,y^{(a}\,\mathcal{P}_{cd}{}^{b)e}y^{c}y^{d}p_{e}\,\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\sigma+\dots\,,

(B.2)

plus terms of order
3
and higher in
y
, but all linear in
p
.
In order to compute the gauge transformation
of
F=\tau(p^{2}+\alpha\,R)
generated by

\xi_{\text{Weyl}}
, and
w_{\text{Weyl}}
given by

w_{\text{Weyl}}=\beta\,\tau(\sigma)=\beta\,\big(1+y^{a}\,\nabla_{a}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}+\dots\big)\,\sigma\,,

(B.3)

one needs to compute the star-product between elements
of the Weyl algebra which are at most quadratic in
p
.
For our purpose, it will be enough to compute
neglecting terms with less, or as many,
y
’s than
p
’s.
We therefore only need the lift of
p^{2}
and
R

up to order
2
in
y
,

\tau(p^{2})=p^{2}+\tfrac{1}{3}\,y^{a}y^{b}\,R_{a}{}^{c}{}_{b}{}^{d}\,p_{c}p_{d}+\dots\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\tau(R)=R+y^{a}\,\nabla_{a}R+\tfrac{1}{2}\,y^{a}y^{b}\,\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}R+\dots\,,

(B.4)

which yields

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{\hbar}\,\big[p^{2},\xi_{\text{Weyl}}\big]_{\ast}

\displaystyle=2\sigma\,p^{2}+4\,\mathcal{P}_{bc}{}^{da}\,y^{b}\,p^{c}p_{d}\,\nabla_{a}\sigma+\tfrac{2}{3}\,y^{c}y^{d}\,\mathcal{P}_{cd}{}^{a(\bullet}\,p^{b)}\,p_{a}\,\nabla_{b}\nabla_{\bullet}\sigma

(B.5)

\displaystyle\hskip 150.0pt+\tfrac{4}{3}\,y^{(b}\,\mathcal{P}_{de}{}^{c)a}\,y^{e}\,p^{d}p_{a}\,\nabla_{b}\nabla_{c}\sigma+\dots

(B.6)

\displaystyle=2\,p^{2}\,\big(\sigma+y^{a}\,\nabla_{a}\sigma+\tfrac{1}{3}\,y^{a}y^{b}\,\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\sigma\big)+\tfrac{2}{3}\,(y\cdot p\,y^{a}-\tfrac{1}{2}\,y^{2}\,p^{a})\,p^{b}\,\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\sigma+\dots\,,

(B.7)

while the commutator of
\xi_{\text{Weyl}}
with other terms
in the lift of
p^{2}
or
R
do not contribute terms
with less
y
’s than
p
’s, and

\displaystyle\big\{\tau(p^{2}),w_{\text{Weyl}}\big\}_{\ast}

\displaystyle=\Big(2\,\big[p^{2}+\tfrac{1}{3}\,R_{a}{}^{c}{}_{b}{}^{d}\,y^{a}y^{b}\,p_{c}p_{d}\big]+\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\,\big[\eta^{ab}+\tfrac{1}{3}\,R_{a}{}^{c}{}_{b}{}^{d}\,y^{a}y^{b}\big]\,\tfrac{\partial^{2}}{\partial y^{a}\partial y^{b}}\big)w_{\text{Weyl}}+\dots

(B.8)

\displaystyle=2\beta\,p^{2}\,\big(\sigma+y^{a}\,\nabla_{a}\sigma+\tfrac{1}{2}\,y^{a}y^{b}\,\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\sigma\big)+\tfrac{2\beta}{3}\,\sigma\,R_{a}{}^{c}{}_{b}{}^{d}\,y^{a}y^{b}\,p_{c}p_{d}+\beta\,\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\,\Box\sigma+\dots

(B.9)

\displaystyle\big\{\tau(R),w_{\text{Weyl}}\big\}_{\ast}

\displaystyle=2\,\tau(R)\,w_{\text{Weyl}}=2\beta\,\sigma\,R+\dots

(B.10)

where again the dots denote terms of order
3

or higher in
y
.
Putting everything together, we end up with

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi_{\text{Weyl}},w_{\text{Weyl}}}F

\displaystyle=2\sigma\,\big[(\beta+1)\,p^{2}+\alpha\beta\,R\big]+\beta\,\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\,\Box\sigma+2(\beta+1)\,p^{2}\,y^{a}\,\nabla_{a}\sigma

(B.11)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+y^{a}y^{b}\,p_{c}p_{d}\,\big([\beta+\tfrac{2}{3}]\,\eta^{cd}\,\delta^{\times}_{a}\delta^{\bullet}_{b}+\tfrac{2}{3}\,\eta^{\times c}\,\delta^{d}_{a}\delta^{\bullet}_{b}-\tfrac{1}{3}\,\eta_{\mathsf{AB}}\,\eta^{\times c}\eta^{\bullet d}\big)\,\nabla_{\times}\nabla_{\bullet}\sigma+\dots

(B.12)

whose value at
y=0
, which we gave earlier in (
4.5.15
),
can be compared to the Weyl variation of
p^{2}+\alpha\,R

and imposing that the two agree implies

\alpha=\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{4(D-1)}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\beta=-1\,.

(B.13)

From now on, we will fix these values,
and will denote the gauge transformations
generated by
\xi_{\text{Weyl}}
and
w_{\text{Weyl}}

with the same symbol as for a Weyl transformation
generated by
\sigma
,

\delta_{\xi_{\text{Weyl}},w_{\text{Weyl}}}\equiv\delta^{\text{Weyl}}_{\sigma}\,,

(B.14)

since the two agree with the aforementioned values
of
\alpha
and
\beta
.
As a final cross-check, let us compute
the Weyl transformation of the equation of motion

\widehat{f}\phi=\big(\Box-\tfrac{D-2}{4(D-1)}\,R\big)\,\phi\,,

(B.15)

which, in our formalism, is obtained by evaluating

\delta^{\text{Weyl}}_{\sigma}\big(\rho(F)\,\Phi\big)=\rho(\delta^{\text{Weyl}}_{\sigma}F)\,\Phi+\rho(F)\,\delta^{\text{Weyl}}_{\sigma}\Phi\,,

(B.16)

at
y=0
, where recall that
\Phi=\tau(\phi)

is the lift of
\phi
as a flat section of the Fock bundle,
whose first order in
y
are given in (
4.3.44
).
To compute the first term, we only need to use
the simple quantization formula (
4.5.3
),
to find

\rho(\delta^{\text{Weyl}}_{\sigma}F)\,\Phi\rvert_{y=0}=\hbar^{2}\,\tfrac{D-4}{6}\,\big(\Box\sigma+\tfrac{1}{D-1}\,R\,\sigma\big)\,\phi\,.

(B.17)

To compute the second term, we should also use

\rho(F)\rvert_{y=0}=\hbar^{2}\,\eta^{ab}\,\tfrac{\partial^{2}}{\partial y^{a}\partial y^{b}}-\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{12}\,\tfrac{D-4}{D-1}\,R\,,

(B.18)

as well as

\tfrac{1}{\hbar}\,\rho(\xi_{\text{Weyl}})=-\big(\sigma+y^{a}\,\nabla_{a}\sigma\big)\,\big(y\cdot\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y}+\tfrac{D}{2}\big)+\tfrac{1}{2}\,y^{2}\,\nabla^{a}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y^{a}}-\tfrac{D+1}{3}\,y^{a}y^{b}\,\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\sigma+\tfrac{1}{6}\,y^{2}\,\Box\sigma+\dots\,,

(B.19)

and

\rho(w_{\text{Weyl}})=-\big(1+y^{a}\,\nabla_{a}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,[\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}-\tfrac{1}{6}\,R_{ab}]+\dots\big)\sigma\,.

(B.20)

Acting with the last two operators on the lift
of
\phi
, one finds

\displaystyle\delta^{\text{Weyl}}_{\sigma}\Phi

\displaystyle=-\rho(\tfrac{1}{\hbar}\,\xi_{\text{Weyl}}+w_{\text{Weyl}})\,\Phi

(B.21)

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{D-2}{2}\,\sigma\,\phi-\tfrac{D}{2}\,y^{a}\nabla_{a}(\sigma\,\phi)-\tfrac{D+2}{4}\,y^{a}y^{b}\,\sigma\,\big(\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}-\tfrac{1}{6}\,R\big)\,\phi

(B.22)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt-\tfrac{D}{2}\,y^{a}y^{b}\,\nabla_{a}\sigma\nabla_{b}\phi-\tfrac{D-2}{6}\,y^{a}y^{b}\,\phi\,\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\sigma+\tfrac{1}{2}\,y^{2}\,\big(\nabla\sigma\cdot\nabla\phi+\tfrac{1}{6}\,\phi\,\Box\sigma\big)+\dots\,,

(B.23)

which leads to

\rho(F)\,\delta^{\text{Weyl}}_{\sigma}\Phi\rvert_{y=0}=-\hbar^{2}\,\tfrac{D+2}{2}\,\sigma\,\Box\phi-\hbar^{2}\,\tfrac{D-4}{6}\,\phi\,\Box\sigma+\hbar^{2}\,\tfrac{D(3D-4)+4}{24(D-1)}\,\sigma\,R\,\phi\,.

(B.24)

Collecting the two terms (
B.17
)
and (
B.24
), we finally obtain the action
of a Weyl transformation on the equation of motion,

\delta^{\text{Weyl}}_{\sigma}\big(\widehat{f}\phi)=-\tfrac{D+2}{2}\,\sigma\,\big(\Box-\tfrac{D-4}{4(D-1)}\,R\big)\,\phi\,,

(B.25)

as expected: we recover the fact that the conformal Laplacian sends functions of Weyl weight

-\tfrac{D-2}{2}
to functions of Weyl weight
-\tfrac{D+2}{2}
.

C
Feigin–Felder–Shoikhet Invariant Trace

The Hochschild cohomology of the Weyl algebra
\mathcal{A}_{2n}

with values in its linear dual
\mathcal{A}^{*}_{2n}

is known to be concentrated in degree
2n
and to be
one-dimensional
[
277
]
.
A representative for this cohomology class,
that we will denote by
\Phi
hereafter,
was given explicitly by Feigin, Felder and Shoikhet

[
246
]
, and reads as follows:

\displaystyle\Phi(a_{0}|a_{1},\dots,a_{2n})

\displaystyle=\int_{u\in\Delta_{2n}}\exp[\hbar\sum_{0\leq i<j\leq 2n}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}+u_{i}-u_{j}\big)\,\pi_{ij}\Big]\,\det|\tfrac{\partial}{\partial p^{I}_{a}},\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y^{a}_{I}}\big|_{I=1,\dots,2n}

(C.1)

\displaystyle\hskip 150.0pt\times\ a_{0}(y_{0},p_{0})\,a_{1}(y_{1},p_{1})\dots a_{2n}(y_{2n},p_{2n})\rvert_{y_{k}=0}\,,

where
\Delta_{2n}
is the standard
2n
-simplex
which can be defined as

\Delta_{2n}=\big\{(u_{1},\dots,u_{2n})\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}\mid u_{0}\equiv 0\leq u_{1}\leq u_{2}\leq\dots\leq u_{2n}\leq 1\big\}\,,

(C.2)

and

\pi_{ij}:=\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{a}_{i}}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{a}^{j}}-\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{a}^{i}}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{a}_{j}}\,,

(C.3)

and the determinant is taken over the
2n\times 2n
matrix
whose entries are the operators

\tfrac{\partial}{\partial p^{I}_{a}}

and
\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y^{a}_{I}}

where the index
I
runs over
1
to
2n
,
so that the argument
a_{0}
remains unaffected
by this determinant operator.

In practice, we need only the Chevalley–Eilenberg cocycle
obtained from
\Phi
by skew-symmetrisation of its arguments,
15
15

15

Recall that the skew-symmetrisation map
is a morphism of complexes between the Hochschild
complex of an associative algebra,
and the Chevalley–Eilenberg of its commutator Lie algebra.

which we will denote by,

[\Phi](a_{0}|a_{1},\dots,a_{2n})=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{2n}}(-1)^{\sigma}\,\Phi(a_{0}|a_{\sigma_{1}},\dots,a_{\sigma_{2n}})\,,

(C.4)

where
(-1)^{\sigma}
denotes the signature
of the permutation
\sigma
. The
n
-cochain defined by

\mu(a_{0}|a_{1},\dots,a_{n}):=\tfrac{1}{n!}\,\epsilon_{b_{1}\dots b_{n}}\,[\Phi](a_{0}|y^{b_{1}},\dots,y^{b_{n}},a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\,,

(C.5)

is
almost
a Chevalley–Eilenberg cocycle,
in the sense that it satisfies

\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}\,\mu\big([a_{-1},a_{i}]_{\ast}|a_{0},\dots,a_{n}\big)+\sum_{0\leq i<j\leq n}(-1)^{i+j}\,\mu\big(a_{-1}|[a_{i},a_{j}]_{\ast},a_{0},\dots,a_{n}\big)=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial p_{a}}\,\varphi_{a}(a_{-1}|a_{0},\dots,a_{n})\,,

(C.6)

where

\varphi_{a}(a_{-1}|a_{0},\dots,a_{n})=\tfrac{1}{(n-1)!}\,\epsilon_{ab_{1}\dots b_{n-1}}\,[\Phi](a_{-1}|y^{b_{1}},\dots,y^{b_{n-1}},a_{0},\dots,a_{n})\,,

(C.7)

i.e. it verifies the cocycle condition modulo
a total derivative in
p
. As a first step towards
simplifying the expression of
\mu
, let us note that

\det|\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y^{a}_{I}},\tfrac{\partial}{\partial p_{a}^{I}}\big|=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n|n}}(-1)^{\sigma}\,\epsilon^{a_{1}\dots a_{n}}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{a_{1}}_{\sigma_{1}}}\dots\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{a_{n}}_{\sigma_{n}}}\,\epsilon_{b_{1}\dots b_{n}}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{b_{1}}^{\sigma_{n+1}}}\dots\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{b_{n}}^{\sigma_{2n}}}\,

(C.8)

where
\mathcal{S}_{n|n}
denotes the set of permutations
of
2n
elements which preserve the order of the first
n

and the last
n
elements separately,
i.e.
\sigma_{1}<\sigma_{2}<\dots<\sigma_{n}

and
\sigma_{n+1}<\sigma_{n+2}<\dots<\sigma_{2n}
, and

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{n!}\,\epsilon_{a_{1}\dots a_{n}}\,

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{2n}}(-1)^{\sigma}\,y^{a_{1}}_{\sigma_{1}}\dots y^{a_{n}}_{\sigma_{n}}\,a_{1}(y_{\sigma_{n+1}},p_{\sigma_{n+1}})\dots a_{n}(y_{\sigma_{2n}},p_{\sigma_{2n}})

(C.9)

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{a_{1}\dots a_{n}}\,\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n|n}}(-1)^{\sigma}\,y^{a_{1}}_{\sigma_{1}}\dots y^{a_{n}}_{\sigma_{n}}\,\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}(-1)^{\tau}\,a_{\tau_{1}}(y_{\sigma_{n+1}},p_{\sigma_{n+1}})\dots a_{\tau_{n}}(y_{\sigma_{2n}},p_{\sigma_{2n}})\,,

so that, put together, these two formulae yield

\displaystyle\det|\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y^{a}_{I}},\tfrac{\partial}{\partial p_{a}^{I}}\big|

\displaystyle\Big(\tfrac{1}{n!}\,\epsilon_{a_{1}\dots a_{n}}\,\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{2n}}(-1)^{\sigma}\,y^{a_{1}}_{\sigma_{1}}\dots y^{a_{n}}_{\sigma_{n}}a_{1}(y_{\sigma_{n+1}},p_{\sigma_{n+1}})\dots a_{n}(y_{\sigma_{2n}},p_{\sigma_{2n}})\Big)

(C.10)

\displaystyle=(2n)!\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{i_{1}<\dots<i_{n}\}\\
\subset\{1,\dots,2n\}\end{subarray}}\ \sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}(-1)^{\sigma}\,\epsilon_{a_{1}\dots a_{n}}\,\frac{\partial a_{\sigma_{1}}}{\partial p_{a_{1}}}(y_{i_{1}},p_{i_{1}})\dots\frac{\partial a_{\sigma_{n}}}{\partial p_{a_{n}}}(y_{i_{n}},p_{i_{n}})\,,

(C.11)

where the first sum is taken over all
ordered
subsets
of
n
integers in the set
\{1,\dots,2n\}
.
We are now in position of writing down the cochain
\mu
:
for any
a_{0},a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in\mathcal{A}_{2n}
,
it is given explicitly by

\mu(a_{0}|a_{1},\dots,a_{n})=(2n)!\,\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}(-1)^{\sigma}\,\int_{u\in\Delta_{2n}}\mathscr{D}(u;a_{0},a_{\sigma_{1}},\dots,a_{\sigma_{n}})\rvert_{y=0}\,,

(C.12)

where

\mathscr{D}(u;-)=\sum_{f\in\Delta([n],[2n])}\ \exp[\hbar\sum_{0\leq i<j\leq n}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}+u_{f(i)}-u_{f(j)}\big)\,\pi_{ij}\Big]\epsilon_{a_{1}\dots a_{n}}\,\big(1\otimes\tfrac{\partial}{\partial p_{a_{1}}}\otimes\dots\otimes\tfrac{\partial}{\partial p_{a_{n}}}\big)\,,

(C.13)

and

\Delta([k],[l]):=\big\{f:\{1,2,\dots,k\}\longrightarrow\{1,2,\dots,l\}\mid f(i)<f(j)\,,\ 1\leq i<j\leq k\big\}

(C.14)

denotes the set of order-preserving maps from the set

[k]
of the first
k
integers, to the set
[l]

of the first
l
integers. Note that by convention,
we put
f(0)=0
and
u_{0}=0
.

Trace on the deformed algebra of functions.

Suppose that
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
are linear in
p
,
and write
\tfrac{\partial a}{\partial p_{b}}=a^{b}(y)

for their derivative with respect to
p
.
Then the above operator collapses to

\mathscr{D}(u;a_{0},a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})=\sum_{f\in\Delta([n],[2n])}\exp[\hbar\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(u_{f(i)}-\tfrac{1}{2}\big)\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial p_{a}}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}^{a}}\Big]\,a_{0}(y,p)\times\epsilon_{b_{1}\dots b_{n}}\,a^{b_{1}}_{1}(y_{1})\dots a^{b_{n}}_{n}(y_{n})\,,

(C.15)

thereby exhibiting a clear distinction between
the arguments: the zeroth one will only receive
derivative with respect to
p
, while the remaining

n
arguments will only receive derivatives
with respect to
y
.
Now consider the case where
a_{0}=F(y,p)
,
and all other arguments are equal to the Fedosov connection,

a_{1}=\dots=a_{n}=A
.
Since
A
is linear in
p
we can write it as

A(y,p)=\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\,e_{\mu}^{a}\,A_{a}{}^{b}(y)\,p_{b}\,,

(C.16)

and introducing the notation

\mathbb{A}(y_{1},\dots,y_{n}):=\epsilon^{a_{1}\dots a_{n}}\,\epsilon_{b_{1}\dots b_{n}}\,A_{a_{1}}{}^{b_{1}}(y_{1})\cdots A_{a_{n}}{}^{b_{n}}(y_{n})\,,

(C.17)

we end up with

\mathscr{D}(u;F,A,\dots,A)=\mathrm{d}^{D}x\,|e|\,\sum_{f\in\Delta([n],[2n])}\exp[\hbar\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(u_{f(i)}-\tfrac{1}{2}\big)\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial p_{a}}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}^{a}}\Big]\,F(y,p)\times\mathbb{A}(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})\,,

(C.18)

where
|e|
is the determinant of the vielbein.
This formula exhibits a couple of properties:

•

First, as we noticed earlier, the argument
F(y,p)

is the only one to receive derivatives with respect to
p
.
This means that in order to compute
\mu(F|A,\dots,A)
,
one only needs to know
F\rvert_{y=0}
, the
y
-independent
part of the symbol
F
.

•

Second, the integral over the simplex will produce
some combinatorial coefficients

\sum_{f\in\Delta([n],[2n])}\int_{\Delta_{2n}}\big(u_{f(\ell_{1})}-\tfrac{1}{2}\big)\,\cdots\,\big(u_{f(\ell_{k})}-\tfrac{1}{2}\big)\,,

(C.19)

which depends on a
k
-tuple of integers

(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{k})
comprised between
1
and
n
.
In fact, one can refine this dependency a little bit
by remarking that if two
k
-tuple are related by
a permutation
\tau\in\mathcal{S}_{k}
, the associated
coefficients are equal,
so that these coefficients may as well be labeled
by partitions of
k
.

Putting this together, one ends up with

\displaystyle\mu(F|A,\dots,A)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}^{D}x\,|e|\,\sum_{k\geq 0}\mu^{\scriptscriptstyle\nabla}_{a_{1}\dots a_{k}}(x)\frac{\partial^{k}}{\partial p_{a_{1}}\cdots\partial p_{a_{k}}}F(y,p)\big|_{y=0}\,,

(C.20)

where
\mu^{\scriptscriptstyle\nabla}_{a_{1}\dots a_{k}}(x)

are polynomials in the (covariant derivatives of the) curvature
of
\nabla
, which is obtained by computing the term
of order
\hbar^{k}
in

\sum_{f\in\Delta([n],[2n])}\int_{\Delta_{2n}}\exp[\hbar\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(u_{f(i)}-\tfrac{1}{2}\big)\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y_{i}^{a}}\otimes\tfrac{\partial}{\partial p_{a}}\Big]\,\mathbb{A}(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})\otimes F(y,p)\big|_{y_{i}=0}\,.

(C.21)

D
Curvature expansion

Since the components of
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
are constructed
from the curvature tensor of
\nabla
, its covariant
derivatives and contractions thereof, we can rearrange
its expansion in the power of the curvature, which appears through

\mathcal{R}\equiv-\tfrac{1}{3}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\,R_{\mu a}{}^{c}{}_{b}\,y^{a}y^{b}\,p_{c}\,,

(D.1)

namely we write
\boldsymbol{\gamma}=\sum_{k\geq 1}\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(k)}

where
\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(k)}
is of order
k
in
\mathcal{R}

and its derivatives. Let us now evaluate
its defining equation (
4.3.18
)
at order
n
in
\mathcal{R}
,

\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(k)}=\mathcal{R}\,\delta_{k,1}+\partial_{\scriptscriptstyle\nabla}\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(k)}+\sum_{l=1}^{k-1}\tfrac{1}{2\hbar}\,h\big[\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(k-l)}\big]_{\ast}\,,

(D.2)

which we can re-write, for
k>1
, as

\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(k)}=\tfrac{1}{2\hbar}\,\sum_{l=1}^{k-1}j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}h\,\big[\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(l)},\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(k-l)}\big]_{\ast}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\text{with}\hskip 20.00003ptj_{\scriptstyle\nabla}:=\frac{1}{1-\partial_{\scriptscriptstyle\nabla}}=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\partial_{\scriptscriptstyle\nabla}^{m}\,.

(D.3)

The first few orders in this curvature expansion

\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(1)}=j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}\mathcal{R}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(2)}=\tfrac{1}{2\hbar}\,j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}\boldsymbol{\{}\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\}}\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(3)}=\tfrac{1}{2\hbar^{2}}\,j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}\boldsymbol{\{}\mathcal{R},\boldsymbol{\{}\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\}}\boldsymbol{\}}\,,

(D.4)

\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(4)}=\tfrac{1}{2\hbar^{3}}\,j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}\boldsymbol{\{}\mathcal{R},\boldsymbol{\{}\mathcal{R},\boldsymbol{\{}\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\}}\boldsymbol{\}}\boldsymbol{\}}+\tfrac{1}{8\hbar^{3}}\,j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}\boldsymbol{\{}\boldsymbol{\{}\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\}},\boldsymbol{\{}\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\}}\boldsymbol{\}}\,,

(D.5)

where we introduce the bracket

\boldsymbol{\{}-,-\boldsymbol{\}}:=h\,\big[j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}(-),j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}(-)\big]_{\ast}\,,

(D.6)

as a shorthand notation. This approach has a couple
of advantages: first, it allows us to access in one go
whole pieces of
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
at arbitrary order in
y
,
and second, the recursion in order of curvature
exhibits an interesting structure, namely it appears
that it is controlled by the grafting (non-planar)
binary trees. Indeed, denoting the operator
j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}

by an edge, and the composition of the star-commutator
with the contracting homotopy by a vertex, i.e.

one can re-write the recursion relation (

(D.9)

for all
k>1
. Now it becomes relatively easy
to see that the result of this recursion relation
is to express
\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(k)}
as a sum
over all
rooted planar binary trees with
k
leaves.

Indeed, the latter can be obtained by successively

grafting
—meaning summing over all trees
resulting from attaching the root of a tree to the leaves
of another one—the rooted binary tree with a single vertex
to itself,
k
times, which is exactly what the above relation
produces. Taking into account the fact that
\boldsymbol{\{}-,-\boldsymbol{\}}

is antisymmetric amounts to identifying any two rooted
planar binary trees which can be related by permuting
the two leaves at each node, that is, one should
sum over rooted
non-planar
binary trees
and take into account the number of planar ones
that it is equivalent to as a multiplicity.

Having worked out the recursion formula for
\boldsymbol{\gamma}

in order of the curvature, we can do the same
for the lift of symbols. Indeed, resumming the defining
relation (
4.3.22
) for the lift
F(x;y,p)

of a symbol
f(x,p)
yields,

F=\partial_{\scriptscriptstyle\nabla}F+\tfrac{1}{\hbar}\,h\,[\boldsymbol{\gamma},F]_{\ast}

(D.10)

from which we can extract the order
k
piece via

F^{(0)}=j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}f\,,\hskip 20.00003ptF^{(k>0)}=\tfrac{1}{\hbar}\,\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}h\,[\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(k-l)},F^{(l)}]_{\ast}\,.

(D.11)

The first few orders are given by

F^{(1)}=\tfrac{1}{\hbar}\,j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}\,\boldsymbol{\{}\mathcal{R},f\boldsymbol{\}}\,,\hskip 20.00003ptF^{(2)}=\tfrac{1}{2\hbar^{2}}\,j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}\Big(\boldsymbol{\{}\boldsymbol{\{}\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\}},f\boldsymbol{\}}+2\,\boldsymbol{\{}\mathcal{R},\boldsymbol{\{}\mathcal{R},f\boldsymbol{\}}\boldsymbol{\}}\Big)\,,

(D.12)

and

F^{(3)}=\tfrac{1}{2\hbar^{3}}\,j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}\Big(\boldsymbol{\{}\boldsymbol{\{}\mathcal{R},\boldsymbol{\{}\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\}}\boldsymbol{\}},f\boldsymbol{\}}+\boldsymbol{\{}\boldsymbol{\{}\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\}},\boldsymbol{\{}\mathcal{R},f\boldsymbol{\}}\boldsymbol{\}}+\boldsymbol{\{}\mathcal{R},\boldsymbol{\{}\boldsymbol{\{}\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\}},f\boldsymbol{\}}\boldsymbol{\}}+2\,\boldsymbol{\{}\mathcal{R},\boldsymbol{\{}\mathcal{R},\boldsymbol{\{}\mathcal{R},f\boldsymbol{\}}\boldsymbol{\}}\boldsymbol{\}}\Big)\,.

(D.13)

Finally, the same can be done for the lift of a function

\phi(x)
to a covariantly constant section
\Phi(x;y)

of the Fock bundle: the re-summed for of the recursion
relation (
4.3.42
) reads

\Phi=\partial_{\scriptscriptstyle\nabla}\Phi+\tfrac{1}{\hbar}\,h\,\rho(\boldsymbol{\gamma})\Phi\,.

(D.14)

which, when evaluated at order
n
in
\mathcal{R}

gives us

\Phi^{(0)}=j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}\,\phi\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\Phi^{(k>0)}=\tfrac{1}{\hbar}\,\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}h\,\rho(\boldsymbol{\gamma}^{(k-l)})\Phi^{(l)}\,.

(D.15)

The first few orders read

\Phi^{(1)}=\tfrac{1}{\hbar}\,j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}\big(\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\triangleright}\phi\big)\,,\hskip 20.00003pt\Phi^{(2)}=\tfrac{1}{2\hbar^{2}}\,j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}\Big(\boldsymbol{\{}\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\}}\boldsymbol{\triangleright}\phi+2\,\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\triangleright}\big(\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\triangleright}\phi\big)\Big)\,,

(D.16)

and

\Phi^{(3)}=\tfrac{1}{2\hbar^{3}}\,j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}\Big(\boldsymbol{\{}\mathcal{R},\boldsymbol{\{}\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\}}\boldsymbol{\}}\boldsymbol{\triangleright}\phi+\boldsymbol{\{}\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\}}\boldsymbol{\triangleright}(\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\triangleright}\phi)+\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\triangleright}\big(\boldsymbol{\{}\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\}}\boldsymbol{\triangleright}\phi\big)+2\,\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\triangleright}\big(\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\triangleright}(\mathcal{R}\boldsymbol{\triangleright}\phi)\big)\Big)\,,

(D.17)

where we introduced the shorthand notation

\bullet\boldsymbol{\triangleright}(-):=h\,\rho\big(j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}(\bullet)\big)\,j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}(-)\,,

(D.18)

for the sakes of conciseness. The term of order
k

will be almost identical to that of
F
,
except for the replacement of
f
with
\phi
,
and any bracket of the form
\boldsymbol{\{}-,f\boldsymbol{\}}
,
i.e. whose second argument is
f
, with
(-)\boldsymbol{\triangleright}\phi
.
This is of course not surprising since the only
difference between the two case is the representation
of the Weyl algebra in which the covariantly constant
section that we are solving for sits in: the adjoint
for symbols like
f
and the Fock one for functions like
\phi
.

Simplifying the elementary operations.

Let us try to find a concise expression for the operator
j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}
.
To do so, first notice that

\partial_{\scriptscriptstyle\nabla}\alpha=h\nabla\alpha=\tfrac{1}{N}\,\big(y^{a}\nabla_{a}\alpha-\nabla\,N\,h\alpha\big)\,,

(D.19)

so that, on forms valued in the Weyl algebra
which are
annihilated
by the homotopy operator
h
,
one finds

h\alpha=0\hskip 20.00003pt\Longrightarrow\hskip 20.00003pt\partial_{\scriptscriptstyle\nabla}\alpha=\tfrac{1}{N}\,y\cdot\nabla\alpha=y\cdot\nabla\,\tfrac{1}{N+1}\,\alpha\,,

(D.20)

where we used the fact that
y\cdot\nabla
is obviously
of degree
1
in
y
, and hence increases the eigenvalue
of the number operator
N
, a fact we should take into account
when moving the latter to the right of the former.
Repeating this operation, we end up with

j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}=\sum_{k\geq 0}(y\cdot\nabla)^{k}\,\tfrac{1}{(N+1)_{k}}=\sum_{k\geq 0}\tfrac{1}{k!}\,(y\cdot\nabla)^{k}\,\tfrac{(1)_{k}}{(N+1)_{k}}\equiv{}_{1}F_{1}\big[1;N+1;y\cdot\nabla\big]\,,

(D.21)

where
(a)_{k}=a(a+1)\dots(a+k-1)
is the raising Pochhammer symbol,
and where we used the fact that
(1)_{k}=k!
to recognize
the
confluent hypergeometric function
.
Let us stress that the above expression is valid only
for
j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}
acting on elements in
{\rm Ker}(h)
,
which is enough for us since we are interested in applying it
to either
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
or a
0
-form, both annihilated by
h
,
by definition.

We can now use the integral representation of the confluent
hypergeometric function,

{}_{1}F_{1}\big[a;b;x\big]:=\sum_{k\geq 0}\tfrac{x^{k}}{k!}\,\tfrac{(a)_{k}}{(b)_{k}}=\frac{\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(a)\,\Gamma(b-a)}\,\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\,e^{t\,x}\,t^{a-1}\,(1-t)^{b-a-1}\,,

(D.22)

which holds for
\real(b)>\real(a)>0
. In our case,
both parameters are positive integer, and verify the inequality
except if
N=0
, i.e. if we act on a
y
-independent
0
-form.
This case is particularly simple to treat since the hypergeometric
series collapses to an ordinary exponential, i.e.

j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}\rvert_{\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(T^{*}\mathscr{X})}=e^{y\cdot\nabla}\,.

(D.23)

We can therefore exclude this case, that is consider
N>0
,
and use the above integral representation to re-write
j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}
as

j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}=N\,\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\,e^{t\,y\cdot\nabla}\,(1-t)^{N-1}=\int_{0}^{1}\,\mathrm{d}t\,e^{(1-t)\,y\cdot\nabla}\,\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,t^{N}\,,

(D.24)

where we used the change of variable
t\to 1-t

before recognizing the derivative. We therefore find

\displaystyle j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\,e^{(1-t)\,y\cdot\nabla}\,\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\alpha(x,t\,\mathrm{d}x,t\,y,p)

(D.25)

\displaystyle=\alpha+y\cdot\nabla\,\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\,e^{(1-t)\,y\cdot\nabla}\,\alpha(x,t\,\mathrm{d}x,t\,y,p)\,,

(D.26)

for any Weyl algebra-valued form
\alpha
such that
h\alpha=0
.
Remark that this last form obtained by integrating by part, namely

j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}=1-\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\,\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\big(e^{(1-t)\,y\cdot\nabla}\big)\,t^{N}\,,

(D.27)

also makes sense for
N=0
, since it reproduces (
D.23
).

Lifts at first order in curvature.

Let us now use this operator to compute the lift
of symbols and wave functions at first order in curvature.
Starting with the latter, we need to first compute

\displaystyle\rho(j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}\mathcal{R})\,j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}\phi

\displaystyle=\exp\Big(\!-\hbar\,\partial_p \cdot\big[\tfrac 12\,\partial_{y_1} + \partial_{y_2}\big]\Big)\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}u\,u^{2}\,e^{(1-u)\,y_{1}\cdot\nabla}\,\big(-\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\,R_{\mu a}{}^{c}{}_{b}\,y_{1}^{a}y_{1}^{b}\,p_{c}\big)\,\times\,e^{y_{2}\cdot\nabla}\phi\big|_{p=0,y_{1}=y=y_{2}}

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{\hbar}{2}\,\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}u\,u^{2}\,e^{(1-u)\,y\cdot\nabla}\,\big(\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\,R_{\mu a}\,y^{a}\big)\times e^{y\cdot\nabla}\phi+(\cdots)\,,

(D.28)

where the dots denote terms annihilated by the contracting
homotopy
h
. Applying the latter composed with
j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}
,
we end up with

\displaystyle\Phi^{(1)}

\displaystyle\equiv\tfrac{1}{\hbar}\,j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}\,h\,\rho(j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}\mathcal{R})\,j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}\phi=-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\int_{[0,1]^{3}}\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}u\ e^{(1-t)\,y\cdot[\nabla_{1}+\nabla_{2}]}

(D.29)

\displaystyle\hskip 150.0pt\times\ \tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,\Big(s\,t^{2}\,u^{2}\,e^{t\,s\,y\cdot[\nabla_{1}+(1-u)\,\nabla_{2}]}\phi(x_{1})\,R_{ab}(x_{2})\,y^{a}y^{b}\Big)\rvert_{x_{1}=x=x_{2}}\,,

where
\nabla_{i}
denotes the covariant derivative
with respect to
x_{i}
. Evaluating this formula up
to third order in
y
yields

\Phi=\phi+y^{a}\,\nabla_{a}\phi+\tfrac{1}{2}\,y^{a}y^{b}\,\big(\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}-\tfrac{1}{6}\,R_{ab}\big)\phi+\tfrac{1}{6}\,y^{a}y^{b}y^{c}\,\big(\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\nabla_{c}-\tfrac{1}{2}\,R_{ab}\,\nabla_{c}-\tfrac{1}{4}\,\nabla_{a}R_{bc}\big)\phi+(\dots)\,,

(D.30)

as previously derived from the defining recursion relation
for the lift of the wave function
\phi
in the Fock bundle.
We can re-write this lift up to first order in curvature as

\Phi(x;y)=\Big(\tau^{(0)}(y\cdot\nabla_{1})+\tau^{(1)}(y\cdot\nabla_{1},y\cdot\nabla_{2})\,\tfrac{1}{2}\,y^{a}y^{b}\,R_{ab}(x_{2})+\dots\Big)\phi(x_{1})\rvert_{x_{i}=x}\,,

(D.31)

with
\tau^{(0)}(z_{1})=e^{z_{1}}
and

\tau^{(1)}(z_{1},z_{2})=-\int_{[0,1]^{3}}\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}u\ e^{(1-t)\,[z_{1}+z_{2}]}\ \tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,\Big(s\,t^{2}\,u^{2}\,e^{t\,s\,[z_{1}+(1-u)\,z_{2}]}\Big)=-\tfrac{1}{6}\,e^{z_{1}}\,{}_{1}F_{1}\big[2;4;z_{2}\big]

(D.32)

which, upon using the integral representation (
D.22
),
can be expressed as

\tau^{(1)}(z_{1},z_{2})=e^{z_{1}}\,\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\ (t-1)\,t\,e^{t\,z_{2}}\,.

(D.33)

and hence

\Phi(x;y)=\Big(1+\tfrac{1}{2}\,y^{a}y^{b}\,\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\ t\,(t-1)\,e^{t\,y\cdot\nabla}R_{ab}+\dots\Big)\,e^{y\cdot\nabla}\phi\,.

(D.34)

Now let us turn our attention to the piece of first order
in curvature of the lift of
f
, for which we need to compute

\displaystyle[j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}\mathcal{R},j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}f]_{\ast}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma=\pm}\sigma\,\exp\Big(\tfrac{\sigma\hbar}{2}\,
\big[\partial_{y_1} \cdot\partial_{p_2}
- \partial_{p_1} \cdot\partial_{y_2}\big]\Big)

(D.35)

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt\times\,\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}u\,e^{(1-u)\,y_{1}\cdot\nabla}\,\big(-u^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\,R_{\mu\,a}{}^{c}{}_{b}y^{a}_{1}y^{b}_{1}\,p_{1\,c}\big)\times e^{y_{2}\cdot\nabla}f(p_{2})\big|_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1}=y=y_{2}\\
p_{1}=p=p_{2}\end{subarray}}

(D.36)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma=\pm}\sigma\,\exp\Big(\tfrac{\sigma\hbar}{2}\,
\partial_{y_1} \cdot\partial_{p_2}\Big)\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}u\,e^{(1-u)\,y_{1}\cdot\nabla}\big(u^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\,R_{\mu\,a}{}^{c}{}_{b}\,y_{1}^{a}y_{1}^{b}\big)

(D.37)

\displaystyle\hskip 180.0pt\times\,\big(-p_{1\,c}+\tfrac{\sigma\hbar}{2}\,\nabla_{c}\big)\,e^{y_{2}\cdot\nabla}\,f(p_{2})\big|_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1}=y=y_{2}\\
p_{1}=p=p_{2}\end{subarray}}

(D.38)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\hbar}{2}\,\sum_{\sigma=\pm}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}u\ e^{(1-u)[\,y+\frac{\sigma\hbar}{2}\,\partial_{p}]\cdot\nabla}\Big(u^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\,R_{\mu\,a}{}^{c}{}_{b}\,\big[y^{b}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial p_{a}}+\tfrac{\sigma\hbar}{2}\,\tfrac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{a}\partial p_{b}}\big]\Big)

(D.39)

\displaystyle\hskip 150.0pt\times\,\big(-p_{c}^{\prime}+\tfrac{\sigma\hbar}{2}\,\nabla_{c}\big)\,e^{y\cdot\nabla}\,f(p)\rvert_{p^{\prime}=p}+(\dots)\,,

(D.40)

where the dots denote terms that are annihilated by
h
.
Applying it followed by
j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}
, one finds

\displaystyle F^{(1)}

\displaystyle\equiv\tfrac{1}{\hbar}\,j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}h\,[j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}\mathcal{R},j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}f]_{\ast}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\sum_{\sigma=\pm}\int_{[0,1]^{3}}\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}u\ e^{(1-t)\,y\cdot[\nabla_{1}+\nabla_{2}]}

(D.41)

\displaystyle\hskip 150.0pt\times\ \tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\,\Big(t\,u^{2}\,e^{s\,t\,y\cdot[(1-u)\,\nabla_{1}+\nabla_{2}]+\frac{\sigma\hbar}{2}\,(1-u)\,\partial_{p}\cdot\nabla_{1}}\,y^{d}\,R_{da}{}^{c}{}_{b}(x_{1})

(D.42)

\displaystyle\hskip 150.0pt\times\,\big[t\,s\,y^{b}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial p_{a}}+\tfrac{\sigma\hbar}{2}\,\tfrac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{a}\partial p_{b}}\big]\,\big(-p_{c}^{\prime}+\tfrac{\sigma\hbar}{2}\,\nabla_{c}\big)\,f(x_{2},p)\Big)\big|_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1}=x=x_{2}\\
p^{\prime}=p\end{subarray}}\,.

(D.43)

After explicitly performing the integrals as for the lift
of
\phi
, one finds

\displaystyle F^{(1)}(x,p;y)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\,e^{y\cdot\nabla_{1}}\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k,l\geq 0\\
\sigma=\pm\end{subarray}}\big(\tfrac{\sigma\hbar}{2}\big)^{k}\,\tfrac{(\partial_{p}\cdot\nabla_{2})^{k}}{k!}\,\tfrac{(y\cdot\nabla_{2})^{l}}{l!}\,y^{a}\,R_{da}{}^{c}{}_{b}(x_{2})\,\tfrac{1}{(k+1)(l+k+2)(l+k+3)}

(D.44)

\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt\times\,\Big[y^{b}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial p_{d}}+\tfrac{\sigma\hbar}{2}\,\tfrac{l+2k+4}{(k+2)}\,\tfrac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{b}\partial p_{d}}\Big]\,\big(p_{c}^{\prime}-\tfrac{\sigma\hbar}{2}\,\nabla_{c}\big)\,f(x_{1},p)\big|_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{i}=x,\\
p^{\prime}=p\end{subarray}}

(D.45)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\sum_{\sigma=\pm}\int_{[0,1]^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}t\ t\,(1-t)\,e^{y\cdot\nabla_{1}+t\,[y+s\frac{\sigma\hbar}{2}\,\partial_{p}]\cdot\nabla_{2}}\,\big[p^{\prime}-\tfrac{\sigma\hbar}{2}\,\nabla_{1}\big]_{c}

(D.46)

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt\times\,y^{a}\,\Big(y^{b}+\sigma\hbar\,\big[1+\tfrac{t}{2}\,(1-s)\,y\cdot\nabla_{2}\big]\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial p_{b}}\Big)\,R_{da}{}^{c}{}_{b}(x_{2})\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial p_{d}}f(x_{1},p)\big|_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{i}=x,\\
p^{\prime}=p\end{subarray}}\,,

(D.47)

for the piece of first order in curvature of the lift

\tau(f)
of any symbol
f(x,p)
.

Generating function.

Combining the first order lift
\Phi^{(1)}

of the scalar field given in (
D.34
) with the fact that

\rho(j_{\scriptstyle\nabla}f)\rvert_{y=0}=\exp\Big(\!-\hbar\partial_p \cdot\big[\tfrac 12\,\nabla+ \partial_y\big]\Big)f\,\big|_{y=0=p}

(D.48)

for a symbol
f=f(x,p)
, i.e.
y
-independent,
one ends up with

\displaystyle\rho(F^{(0)})\Phi^{(1)}\rvert_{y=0}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\,\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}t\ (t-1)t\,\times\,e^{-\hbar\,\partial_{p}\cdot[\nabla_{1}+t\,\nabla_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\,\nabla_{3}]}\,\phi(x_{1})\,R_{ab}(x_{2})\,\tfrac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{a}\partial p_{b}}f(x_{3},p)\big|_{x_{i}=x,p=0}\,,

(D.49)

upon using the BCH formula. Note that the commutator appearing
in these manipulations can be discarded as a consequence
of the fact that we are working at first order in curvature.
Now applying the quantization map to the first order lift

F^{(1)}
of a symbol given in (
D.46
),
we end up with,

\displaystyle\rho(F^{(1)})\Phi^{(0)}\rvert_{y=0}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{8}\,\sum_{\sigma=\pm}\int_{[0,1]^{2}}\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}t\ t(t-1)\,e^{-\frac{\hbar}{2}\,\partial_{p}\cdot[2\nabla_{1}+t(1-\sigma\,s)\,\nabla_{2}+\nabla_{3}]}

(D.50)

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt\times\,\Big[(1-2\sigma)+\tfrac{\sigma\hbar}{2}\,t(1-s)\,\partial_{p}\cdot\nabla_{2}\Big]\,\phi(x_{1})\,R_{ab}(x_{2})\,\tfrac{\partial^{2}}{\partial p_{a}\partial p_{b}}f(x_{3},p)\big|_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{i}=x,\\
p=0\end{subarray}}\,,

after using the BCH formula a few times as before.
Performing the integrals, multiplying the result by
\phi^{*}
,
and integrating by part so that all derivatives on
f

are re-distributed on
\phi
,
\phi^{*}
and the curvature,
one ends up with

{\cal J}(x|u)=e^{-\frac{\hbar}{2}\,u\cdot[\nabla_{1}-\nabla_{2}]}\,\Big(1-\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{8}\,{\rm sinhc}(\tfrac{\hbar}{4}\,u\cdot\nabla_{3})\,R_{ab}(x_{3})\,u^{a}u^{b}+{\cal O}(R^{2})\Big)\,\phi(x_{1})\,\phi^{\dagger}(x_{2})\big|_{x_{i}=x}\,,

(D.51)

where

{\cal J}(x|u):=\sum_{s\geq 0}\tfrac{(-\hbar)^{s}}{2^{s}\,s!}\,J_{a_{1}\dots a_{s}}(x)\,u^{a_{1}}\dots u^{a_{s}}\,,

(D.52)

is the generating function for the higher spin currents, and

{\rm sinhc}(z):=\frac{\sinh(z)}{z}\,.

(D.53)

is the hyperbolic version of the
\rm sinc
function.
